    and as these terms are summable we can apply Borel-Cantelli to conclude that these deviations happen only finitely often for every $\epsilon>0,$ giving us the almost-sure convergence for   
    \begin{equation} 
    \frac1J\sum_j h(\tilde X_{k_j}^J)\, \frac{U_{k_j}^J}{\pi_{k_j}^J}
    -  \sum_j h(\tilde X_j^J)\, \frac{U_j^J}{\pi_j^J}\, \pi_j^J \stackrel{a.s.}{\to} 0.
    \end{equation} 
    Similarly, we also have that
    \begin{equation} 
    \sum_j \frac{U_j^J}{\pi_j^J}\pi_j^J
    - \frac1J\sum_j \frac{U_{k_j}^J}{\pi_{k_j}^J} \stackrel{a.s.}{\to} 0,
    \end{equation}
    and the result follows by taking the ratio, since $\sum_j U_j^J=1$.

    
    
   % Let $h$ be an integrable function. 
    %\begin{align*}
    %    \E\left[\sum_{j=1}^J h(Y_j) v_j\right] 
    %    &= \sum_{j=1}^J \E\left[h(X_{a(j)}) \frac{u_{a(j)}}{\pi_{a(j)}}\right] \\
    %    &= \sum_{j=1}^J \E\left[h(X_{j}) u_{j}\right],
    %\end{align*}
    %and similarly for the denominator. Now, the numerator and denominator of the reweighted quantity have the same expectation as the numerator and denominator of the original quantity, so they must converge to $ E_\pi(h(X))$ almost surely as well. 
\end{proof}


\noindent \textbf{Remark:} Note that Lemma \ref{lem:change-measure-proper-weights} permits $\pi_{1:J}$ to depend on $\{(X_j,u_j)\}$ as long as the resampling is carried out independently of $\{(X_j,u_j)\}$, conditional on $\pi_{1:J}$.


\begin{lem}[Particle Marginals]
\label{lem:marginal-proper-weights}
Suppose that $\{(\tilde X_j^J,U_j^J),j=1,\dots,J\}$ targets $f_X$.
Also suppose that conditional on $(\tilde X_m^J,U_m^J)_{m=1}^J$,
the variables $\tilde Z_1^J,\ldots,\tilde Z_J^J$ are independent, with
$\tilde Z_j^J\sim f_{Z|X}(\cdot\mid\tilde X_j^J)$,
where $f_{Z|X}$ is a conditional probability density function corresponding
to a joint density $f_{X,Z}$ with marginal densities $f_X$ and $f_Z$.
Then, if the $U_j^J$ are uniformly bounded above and away from zero,
$\{(\tilde Z_j^J,U_j^J)\}$ targets $f_Z$.
\end{lem}

\begin{proof}[Proof.]
We want to show that, for any measurable bounded $h$,
\begin{equation}
 \frac{\sum_j h(\tilde Z_j^J)\,U_j^J}{\sum_j U_j^J}
 \stackrel{a.s.}{\to}\E_{f_Z}[h(Z)]
 =\E_{f_X}\big[\E_{f_{Z|X}}[h(Z)\mid X]\big].
\end{equation}
By assumption, for any measurable and bounded functional $g$ with domain $\gX$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:lemma2:g}
 \frac{\sum_j g(\tilde X_j^J)\,U_j^J}{\sum_j U_j^J}
 \stackrel{a.s.}{\to}\E_{f_X}[g(X)].
\end{equation}
Let $\bar U_j^J=JU_j^J/\sum_j U_j^J$.
For constants $0<c\le U_j^J\le C<\infty$, uniformly in $j,J$,
we have $\bar U_j^J\le C/c$.
Examine the numerator and denominator of the quantity
\begin{equation}
 \frac{J^{-1}\sum_j h(\tilde Z_j^J)\,U_j^J}{J^{-1}\sum_j U_j^J}
 =\frac{J^{-1}\sum_j h(\tilde Z_j^J)\,\bar U_j^J}
 {J^{-1}\sum_j\bar U_j^J}.
\end{equation}
The denominator converges to $1$ almost surely. The numerator, on the other hand, is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:lemma6:numerator}
 \frac1J\sum_j h(\tilde Z_j^J)\,\bar U_j^J.
\end{equation}
Set
\[
 D_J=\frac1J\sum_j\bar U_j^J
 \bigl\{h(\tilde Z_j^J)-\E[h(Z)\mid X=\tilde X_j^J]\bigr\}.
\]
Conditioning on $(\tilde X_j^J,U_j^J)_{j=1}^J$, we apply the fourth-moment
argument of Lemma~\ref{lem:change-measure-proper-weights} to the centered, uniformly bounded summands, obtaining
\[
 \E[D_J^4]\le\frac{C_h}{J^2},\qquad
 \mathbb P(|D_J|>\epsilon)\le\frac{C_h}{\epsilon^4J^2},
\]
for some $C_h<\infty$ independent of $J$ and every $\epsilon>0$.
Thus $D_J\stackrel{a.s.}{\to}0$ by Borel--Cantelli.
For the expectation of (\ref{eq:lemma6:numerator}), the tower property gives
\begin{align}
 \lim_{J\to\infty}\E\left[\frac1J\sum_j h(\tilde Z_j^J)\,\bar U_j^J\right]
 &=\lim_{J\to\infty}\E\left[\frac1J\sum_j
 \E\big[h(\tilde Z_j^J)\,\bar U_j^J\mid\tilde X_j^J,\bar U_j^J\big]\right]
 \\
 &=\lim_{J\to\infty}\E\left[\frac1J\sum_j
 \E\big[h(Z)\mid X=\tilde X_j^J\big]\,\bar U_j^J\right].
\end{align}
Applying (\ref{eq:lemma2:g}) with $g(x)=\E[h(Z)\mid X=x]$, the average on the
right hand side converges almost surely to
$\E\{\E[h(Z)\mid X]\}=\E[h(Z)]$.
It remains to swap the limit and expectations. We can do so with the bounded
convergence theorem. The almost-sure convergence of this average,
together with $D_J\stackrel{a.s.}{\to}0$, gives
\begin{equation}
 \frac1J\sum_j h(\tilde Z_j^J)\,\bar U_j^J
 \stackrel{a.s.}{\to}\E_{f_Z}[h(Z)].\qedhere
\end{equation}
\end{proof}

\noindent\textbf{Remark.} The conditional independence assumption in Lemma~\ref{lem:marginal-proper-weights} holds for
standard particle filters, and for our MOP filter it is guaranteed by step 1
of Definition~\ref{defn:off-parameter-filter}. 

\vspace{1mm}

\noindent \textbf{Remark:}  The uniform boundedness condition in Lemma~\ref{lem:marginal-proper-weights} can be guaranteed by construction in the context of Definition~\ref{defn:off-parameter-filter} for suitable choices of the resampling probabilities $\pi_{1:J}$ at an earlier iteration.
In the context of DMOP-$\alpha$, this is guaranteed if the measurement density is similarly bounded, which holds under Assumption (A2) for our theoretical analysis. 
It is trivially true for MOP-$\alpha$ at $\theta=\phi$, since in this case the weights at each propagation step are equal.
The smoothness assumption (A5) therefore also guarantees this condition in a neighborhood of $\theta=\phi$.

\vspace{1mm}

\noindent\textbf{Remark.} For nonnegative weights with positive sum, the weight condition in Lemma~\ref{lem:marginal-proper-weights} can be replaced by the weaker requirement
\[
 \max_{1\le j\le J}\frac{JU_j^J}{\sum_mU_m^J}\le B<\infty,
\]
almost surely, for a constant $B$ independent of $J$. The same proof applies.


\begin{lem}[Particle Posteriors]
    \label{lem:posterior-proper-weights}
    Suppose that $\{(X_j^J,U_j^J),j=1,\dots,J\}$ targets $f_X$.
    Also suppose that $(X^{\prime J}_j,U^{\prime J}_j) = \big(X_j^J,U_j^J \, f_{Z|X}(z^*|X_j^J)\big)$. 
    Then, if $x\mapsto f_{Z|X}(z^*|x)$ is bounded and $0<f_Z(z^*)<\infty$, $\{(X^{\prime J}_j,U^{\prime J}_j)\}$ targets $f_{X|Z}(\cdot | z^*)$.
\end{lem}

\begin{proof}[Proof.]
    Again, we want to show that, for any measurable bounded $h$,
     \begin{equation}
     \frac{\sum_j h(X_j^J) \cdot U_j^J \cdot f_{Z|X}(z^*|X_j^J)}{\sum_j U_j^J \cdot f_{Z|X}(z^*|X_j^J)} \stackrel{a.s.}{\to} \E_{f_{X|Z}}\big[h(X)\big|z^*\big].
     \end{equation}
    We already have that for any measurable bounded $g$,
    \begin{equation}
        \frac{\sum_j g(X_j^J)\, U_j^J}{\sum_j U_j^J} \stackrel{a.s.}{\to} \E_{f_X}\big[g(X)\big].
        \label{eq:lemma-posterior-hypothesis}
    \end{equation}
    Consider the following:
    \begin{equation}
    \frac{J^{-1} \sum_j h(X_j^J) \, f_{Z|X}(z^*|X_j^J)\,  {U}_j^{J}}{J^{-1} \sum_j {U}_j^{J}}
    \times \left( \frac{J^{-1} \sum_j f_{Z|X}(z^*|X_j^J) \, {U}_j^{J}}{J^{-1} \sum_j {U}_j^{J}} \right)^{-1}.
    \end{equation}
    The functions $x\mapsto f_{Z|X}(z^*|x)$ and $x\mapsto h(x)f_{Z|X}(z^*|x)$ are bounded.
    We will apply Equation (\ref{eq:lemma-posterior-hypothesis}) to the numerator and the denominator in the ratio above individually. The numerator converges to 
    \begin{equation}
    \frac{J^{-1} \sum_j h(X_j^J) \, f_{Z|X}(z^*|X_j^J) \, {U}_j^{J}}{J^{-1} \sum_j {U}_j^{J}} \stackrel{a.s.}{\to} \E_{f_X}\big[h(X)\, f_{Z|X}(z^*|X)\big],
    \end{equation}
    while the denominator converges to
    \begin{equation} 
    \frac{J^{-1} \sum_j f_{Z|X}(z^*|X_j^J) \, {U}_j^{J}}{J^{-1} \sum_j {U}_j^{J}}  \stackrel{a.s.}{\to} \E_{f_X}\big[f_{Z|X}(z^*|X)\big] = f_Z(z^*).
    \end{equation}
    Now we take advantage of the identities
    \begin{eqnarray}
    \frac{\E_{f_X}\big[h(X)\, f_{Z|X}(z^*|X)\big]}{f_Z(z^*)} &=& \E_{f_X}\left[\frac{h(X)\, f_{Z|X}(z^*|X)}{f_Z(z^*)}\right] 
    \\
    &=& \E_{f_X}\left[h(X)\frac{f_{X|Z}(X|z^*)}{f_X(X)}\right] =  \E_{f_{X|Z}}\big[h(X)|z^*\big],
    \end{eqnarray}
    to give the desired result.
    
    
    % Write $\bar{U}_j^{J} = \frac{J U_j^J}{\sum_j U_j^J}$. Define the weights $U^{' J}_j := U_j^J \cdot f_{Z|X}(z^*|X_j^J),$ and the corresponding self-normalized weights $\bar{U}_j^{' J} := \frac{J U^{' J}_j}{\sum_j U^{' J}_j}.$ The denominator of the below quantity 
    % \begin{equation} \frac{J^{-1} \sum_j h(X_j^J) \bar{U}_j^{' J}}{J^{-1} \sum_j \bar{U}_j^{' J}}\end{equation}
    % converges to $1$, while the numerator, by the same fourth moment argument as the first lemma, converges almost surely to the limit of its expectation, which is
    % \begin{align*}
    %     \lim_{J\to\infty} \E\left[J^{-1} \sum_j h(X_j^J) \bar{u}_j^{' J} \right]
    %     &= \lim_{J\to\infty} \E\left[J^{-1} \sum_j h(X_j^J) \frac{J u_j^J \cdot f_{Z|X}(z^*|X_j^J)}{\sum_j u_j^J \cdot f_{Z|X}(z^*|X_j^J)}\right] \\
    %     &= \lim_{J\to\infty} \E\left[J^{-1} \sum_j h(X_j^J) \frac{J \frac{u_j^J \cdot f_{Z|X}(z^*|X_j^J)}{f_Z(z^*)}}{\sum_j \frac{u_j^J \cdot f_{Z|X}(z^*|X_j^J)}{f_Z(z^*)}}\right]\\
    %     &= \lim_{J\to\infty} \E\left[ \sum_j h(X_j^J) \frac{\frac{u_j^J \cdot f_{Z|X}(z^*|X_j^J)}{f_Z(z^*)}}{\sum_j \frac{u_j^J \cdot f_{Z|X}(z^*|X_j^J)}{f_Z(z^*)}}\right]\\
    %     &= \E_{X|Z}[h(X)|z^*],
    % \end{align*}

    % where we can swap the limit and expectations with the bounded convergence theorem.
\end{proof}


\noindent\textbf{Remark.} In the MOP application, the boundedness and positivity conditions in Lemma~\ref{lem:posterior-proper-weights} follow from Assumption~\refAssumpBoundedMeasurement.

Further, let $f_{Y|X}$ be a conditional probability density function corresponding to a joint density $f_{X,Y}$ with marginal densities $f_X$ and $f_Y$. \begin{thm}[Off-Parameter Resampled Particle Filters Target the Filtering Distribution]
    \label{thm:off-parameter-targeting}
    The off-parameter resampled particle filter of Definition \ref{defn:off-parameter-filter} targets the filtering distribution when the resampling probabilities $(p_{n,j})_{j=1}^J$ are chosen so that $g_{n,j}/(J\, p_{n,j})$ is uniformly bounded above and away from zero whenever $g_{n,j}>0$.
\end{thm}
\begin{proof}[Proof.]
    Recursively applying Lemmas \ref{lem:change-measure-proper-weights}, \ref{lem:marginal-proper-weights}, and \ref{lem:posterior-proper-weights}, we obtain that the off-parameter resampled particle filter targets the posterior.
    Specifically, suppose inductively that $\big\{\big(X^{F}_{n-1,j},w^{F}_{n-1,j}\big)\big\}$ targets $\pi_{n-1}$.
    Then, Lemma \ref{lem:marginal-proper-weights} tells us that $\big\{\big(X^{P}_{n,j},w^{P}_{n,j}\big)\big\}$ targets $\eta_n$.
    Lemma \ref{lem:posterior-proper-weights} tells us that $\big\{\big(X^{P}_{n,j},w^{P}_{n,j} g^\theta_{n,j} \big)\big\}$ therefore targets  $\pi_n$.
    Lemma \ref{lem:change-measure-proper-weights} guarantees that the resampling rule, given by 
    \begin{equation}
    \big(X^{F}_{n,j},w^{F}_{n,j}\big) = \big(X^{P}_{n,k_j}, w^{P}_{n,k_j} \, g_{n,k_j}\big/ p_{n,k_j}\big),
    \end{equation}
    with resampling probabilities proportional to $p_{n,j}$, therefore also targets $\pi_n$.
\end{proof}

\begin{prop}[MOP-1 Targets the Filtering Distribution]
    When $\alpha=1$ or $\phi=\theta$, MOP-$\alpha$ targets the filtering distribution. 
\end{prop}
\begin{proof}[Proof.]
    When $\theta=\phi$, regardless of the value of $\alpha$, the ratio $\frac{g_{n,j}^\theta}{g_{n,j}^\phi}=1,$ and this reduces to the vanilla particle filter estimate.

    When $\alpha=1$, and $\theta\neq\phi,$ the proof is identical to that of \ref{thm:off-parameter-targeting}. Recursively applying Lemmas \ref{lem:change-measure-proper-weights}, \ref{lem:marginal-proper-weights}, and \ref{lem:posterior-proper-weights}, we obtain that 
    %to step~\ref{mop:step1}, Lemma~2 step~ {mop:weight:update} and Lemma~3 to step~\ref{mop:step2} we obtain that
    the MOP-1 filter targets the posterior.
    Specifically, suppose inductively that $\big\{\big(X^{F,\theta}_{n-1,j},w^{F,\theta}_{n-1,j}\big)\big\}$ is properly weighted for $f_{X_{n-1}|Y_{1:n-1}}(x_{n-1}|y^*_{1:n-1};\theta)$.
    Then, Lemma \ref{lem:marginal-proper-weights} tells us that $\big\{\big(X^{P,\theta}_{n,j},w^{P,\theta}_{n,j}\big)\big\}$ targets $f_{X_{n}|Y_{1:n-1}}(x_{n}|y^*_{1:n-1};\theta)$.
    Lemma \ref{lem:posterior-proper-weights} tells us that $\big\{\big(X^{P,\theta}_{n,j},w^{P,\theta}_{n,j} \, g^\theta_{n,j} \big)\big\}$ therefore targets  $f_{X_{n}|Y_{1:n}}(x_{n}|y^*_{1:n};\theta)$.
    Lemma \ref{lem:change-measure-proper-weights} guarantees that the resampling rule, given by 
    \begin{equation}
    \big(X^{F,\theta}_{n,j},w^{F,\theta}_{n,j}\big) = \big(X^{P,\theta}_{n,k_j}, w^{P,\theta}_{n,k_j} \, g^\theta_{n,k_j}\big/ g^\phi_{n,k_j}\big),
    \end{equation}
    with resampling weights proportional to $g^\phi_{n,j}$, therefore also targets $f_{X_{n}|Y_{1:n}}(x_{n}|y^*_{1:n};\theta)$.
\end{proof}

This has addressed filtering, but not quite yet the likelihood evaluation. For this we use the following lemma.

\begin{lem}[Likelihood Proper Weighting]
    \label{lem:lik-proper-weight}
  $f_{Y_n|Y_{1:n-1}}(y_n^*|y_{1_n-1}^*;\theta)$ is strongly consistently estimated by either the before-resampling estimate,
\begin{equation}\label{L1}
L_n^{B,\theta} =  \frac{\sum_{j=1}^Jg^\theta_{n,j} \, w^{P,\theta}_{n,j}}{\sum_{j=1}^J  w^{P,\theta}_{n,j}},
\end{equation}
or by the after-resampling estimate,
\begin{equation}\label{L2}
L_n^{A,\theta} = L^\phi_n \frac{\sum_{j=1}^Jw^{F,\theta}_{n,j}}{\sum_{j=1}^J  w^{P,\theta}_{n,j}}.
\end{equation}
where $L^\phi_n$ is as defined in the various algorithms.
\end{lem}

Here, (\ref{L1}) is a direct consequence of our earlier result that $\{ \big(X^{P,\theta}_{n,j},w^{P,\theta}_{n,j}\big) \}$ targets\\
$f_{X_{n}|Y_{1:n-1}}(x_{n}|y^*_{1:n-1};\theta)$.
To see (\ref{L2}), define the corrected weights before resampling and the
reference resampling probabilities by
\begin{equation}
w_{n,j}^{FC,\theta}
=w_{n,j}^{P,\theta}\frac{g_{n,j}^\theta}{g_{n,j}^\phi},
\qquad
p_{n,j}=\frac{g_{n,j}^\phi}{JL_n^\phi},
\qquad \sum_{j=1}^Jp_{n,j}=1.
\end{equation}
The numerator of (\ref{L1}) can then be written as
\begin{equation}
\sum_{j=1}^Jw_{n,j}^{P,\theta}g_{n,j}^\theta
=JL_n^\phi\sum_{j=1}^Jp_{n,j}w_{n,j}^{FC,\theta}.
\end{equation}
Resampling selects $w_{n,j}^{F,\theta}=w_{n,k_j}^{FC,\theta}$.
With the pre-resampling particles, weights, and reference densities fixed,
the expectation over multinomial resampling satisfies
\begin{align}
\E_{\mathrm{res}}\left[L_n^\phi\sum_{j=1}^Jw_{n,j}^{F,\theta}\right]
&=JL_n^\phi\sum_{m=1}^Jp_{n,m}w_{n,m}^{FC,\theta} \notag\\*
&=\sum_{m=1}^Jw_{n,m}^{P,\theta}g_{n,m}^\theta.
\end{align}
The same conditional triangular-array argument as in
Lemma~\ref{lem:change-measure-proper-weights} gives consistency of this
resampling approximation under its boundedness conditions. Dividing by
the unchanged prediction-weight sum gives (\ref{L2}).
Using Lemma \ref{lem:lik-proper-weight}, we obtain a likelihood estimate,
\begin{equation}
L^{A,\theta} = \prod_{n=1}^N \left( L^\phi_n \, \frac{\sum_{j=1}^J w^{F,\theta}_{n,j}}{\sum_{j=1}^J w^{P,\theta}_{n,j}}\right).
\end{equation}
Since $w^{F,\theta}_{n,j}=w^{P,\theta}_{n+1,j}$, this is a telescoping product. The remaining terms are
$\sum_{j=1}^J w^{F,\theta}_{0,j} = J$ on the denominator and $\sum_{j=1}^J w^{F,\theta}_{N,j}$ on the numerator.
This derives the MOP-1 likelihood estimates.


$L^{B,\theta}$ should generally be preferred in practice, since there is no reason to include the extra variability from resampling when calculating the conditional log likelihood, but it lacks the nice telescoping product that lets us derive exact expressions for the gradient in Theorem~\thmMopFunctionalForms.



\section{Proof of Theorem~{\thmMopGradConsistency} via Consistency of Off-Parameter Resampled Gradient Estimates}
\label{appendix:consistency}



We now show that, under sufficient local regularity conditions, one can interchange differentiation and the particle limit. We retain the Arzel\`a--Ascoli argument, working on a compact neighborhood of the evaluation parameter. Consistency is used for the estimates themselves: differentiating a weighted particle estimate also differentiates its particles, weights, and normalizer, so boundedness alone does not make its derivative an empirical average to which Theorem \ref{thm:off-parameter-targeting} applies.

\begin{thm}[Off-Parameter Particle Filters Yield Strongly Consistent Estimates of Derivatives of Functionals]
\label{thm:consistent-derivatives}
Let $K$ be a closed ball of positive radius centered at $\phi$ and contained in the interior of $\Theta\subset\mathbb R^p$. Let $F_J(\theta)$ be scalar particle estimates, such as $\eta_n^J(h_\theta)$, defined on a common probability space. On one probability-one event, suppose every $F_J$ has two continuous derivatives on a neighborhood of $K$, with
\[
\sup_{J\geq1}\sup_{\theta\in K}\|\nabla_\theta F_J(\theta)\|_2\leq G^*,
\qquad
\sup_{J\geq1}\sup_{\theta\in K}\|\nabla_\theta^2 F_J(\theta)\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq H^*
\]
for finite constants $G^*,H^*$. Suppose $F_J(\theta)\to h_\theta^*$ almost surely for each fixed $\theta\in K$, where $h^*$ is deterministic. Then $h^*$ is continuously differentiable in the interior of $K$, and almost surely
\[
\nabla_\theta F_J(\theta)\longrightarrow\nabla_\theta h_\theta^*
\]
uniformly on every compact subset of that interior, in particular at $\theta=\phi$.
\end{thm}
\begin{proof}[Proof.]
For each fixed pair $x,y\in K$, pointwise almost-sure convergence and the gradient bound give $|h_y^*-h_x^*|\leq G^*\|y-x\|_2$. Thus the deterministic function $h^*$ is Lipschitz, and in particular continuous, on $K$.

Choose a countable dense subset $D$ of $K$. Intersect the probability-one event of the derivative bounds with the events of convergence at each $\theta\in D$, and fix $\omega$ in this intersection. Suppress $\omega$ below. By the gradient bound, the functions $F_J$ are uniformly Lipschitz on the convex set $K$. Convergence on $D$, compactness of $K$, and continuity of $h^*$ therefore imply $F_J\to h^*$ uniformly on $K$: for any $\epsilon>0$, choose a sufficiently fine finite net in $D$, use convergence at its finitely many points, and use the common Lipschitz bound and uniform continuity of $h^*$ between those points.

The gradients are uniformly bounded and, by the Hessian bound, uniformly equicontinuous. Explicitly, for $\theta,\theta'\in K$,
\begin{equation}
\|\nabla_\theta F_J(\theta)-\nabla_\theta F_J(\theta')\|_2
\leq H^*\|\theta-\theta'\|_2.
\end{equation}
Thus, by Arzel\`a--Ascoli, every subsequence of gradients has a further subsequence converging uniformly on $K$ to a continuous function $g$. We claim that every such limit is $\nabla_\theta h^*$ in the interior of $K$. For $x,y\in K$, the fundamental theorem of calculus gives
\begin{equation}
F_J(y)-F_J(x)
=\int_0^1\nabla_\theta F_J\bigl(x+t(y-x)\bigr)^{\mathsf T}(y-x)\,dt.
\end{equation}
Passing to the limit along the convergent subsequence yields
\begin{equation}
h_y^*-h_x^*
=\int_0^1 g\bigl(x+t(y-x)\bigr)^{\mathsf T}(y-x)\,dt.
\end{equation}
Taking $y=x+te_i$ for each coordinate direction and letting $t\to0$ shows that $\partial_{\theta_i}h_x^*=g_i(x)$ for every interior $x$. Since $g$ is continuous, $h^*$ is continuously differentiable there. Every subsequential gradient limit is therefore the same on the interior, and hence on $K$ by continuity. Relative compactness and uniqueness of this limit imply convergence of the full gradient sequence. In particular, almost surely, for interior $\theta$,
\begin{equation}
\lim_{J\to\infty}\nabla_\theta F_J(\theta)
=\nabla_\theta\lim_{J\to\infty}F_J(\theta)
=\nabla_\theta h_\theta^*,
\end{equation}
with convergence uniform on every compact subset of the interior, as required.
\end{proof}
For $F_J(\theta)=\eta_n^J(h_\theta)$, the conclusion is convergence to $\nabla_\theta\eta_n(h_\theta)$. No identity with $\eta_n(\nabla_\theta h_\theta)$ is asserted, since the target distribution can itself depend on $\theta$.

Theorem~{\thmMopGradConsistency} follows by taking $F_J(\theta)=\hat\ell_J^1(\theta)$ and $h_\theta^*=\ell(\theta)$. Keep the reference parameter $\phi$ fixed throughout $K$. For $\alpha=1$, the off-parameter consistency result and Lemma~\ref{lem:lik-proper-weight} give $\hat\lik_J^1(\theta)\to\lik(\theta)>0$ almost surely for each fixed $\theta\in K$, not only at $\theta=\phi$. The continuous mapping theorem gives the corresponding convergence of the log likelihoods. The neighborhood form of Assumption~\refAssumpLocalBoundedDerivative\ supplies their uniform gradient and Hessian bounds, and Assumption~\refAssumpDifferentiableSimulator\ supplies their pathwise smoothness with the simulator draws and reference-filter resampling indices held fixed. Applying the theorem to these log likelihoods establishes score consistency at $\phi$ directly.


\section{The case $\alpha=0$}
\label{appendix:biasvar}

\kevin{\normalfont\normalsize
At $\alpha=0$, the weight gradient contains only the current measurement
term. This gives a simple preliminary case for the general argument in
Section~\ref{appendix:sharper-biasvar}.
Let $h_{n,j}$ be the full pathwise derivative of
$\log f_{Y_n\mid X_n}(y_n^*\mid X_{n,j}^{P,\theta};\theta)$ at
$\theta=\phi$, including the derivative of the simulated state.
Write $W_{n,j}=g_{n,j}^\phi/\sum_lg_{n,l}^\phi$, and let $K_{n,j}$ be
independent draws from these weights conditional on the history
$\mathcal F_n^-$ just before resampling.
\begin{lem}[Current measurement gradients]
\label{lem:dmop-zero-current-gradient}
At $\theta=\phi$, the before- and after-resampling scores satisfy
\begin{align}
\nabla_\theta\hat\ell_J^{B,0}
&=\sum_{n=1}^N\sum_{j=1}^JW_{n,j}h_{n,j},&
\nabla_\theta\hat\ell_J^{A,0}
&=\sum_{n=1}^N\frac1J\sum_{j=1}^Jh_{n,K_{n,j}}.
\label{eq:dmop-zero-current-score}
\end{align}
Under (A3), their difference has mean zero and
\begin{equation}
\E\left\|\nabla_\theta\hat\ell_J^{A,0}
-\nabla_\theta\hat\ell_J^{B,0}\right\|_2^2
\leq NpG'(\theta)^2/J.
\label{eq:dmop-zero-resampling-difference}
\end{equation}
\end{lem}
\begin{proof}
The prediction weights are one when $\alpha=0$. Differentiating the
weighted measurement average, or its resampled average, gives
Equation~(\ref{eq:dmop-zero-current-score}). Put
$\bar h_n=\sum_jW_{n,j}h_{n,j}$ and
$\varepsilon_n=J^{-1}\sum_jh_{n,K_{n,j}}-\bar h_n$.
Conditional multinomial sampling gives
\begin{align}
\E(\varepsilon_n\mid\mathcal F_n^-)&=0,\notag\\
\E(\|\varepsilon_n\|_2^2\mid\mathcal F_n^-)
&=\frac1J\sum_jW_{n,j}\|h_{n,j}-\bar h_n\|_2^2
\leq\frac{pG'(\theta)^2}{J}.
\end{align}
For $m<n$, $\varepsilon_m$ is $\mathcal F_n^-$-measurable, so the
cross terms in $\E\|\sum_n\varepsilon_n\|_2^2$ vanish. Summing the
conditional bounds proves the result.
\end{proof}
\endgraf
This calculation controls the extra resampling error. Controlling error
relative to the population score also requires control of the particle
histories. Under (A6), we decompose each measurement gradient according
to the age of its parameter dependence; under (A6-J), it is a function
of the current state and its parameter derivative. Lemma~
\ref{lem:dmop-alpha-variance} gives, for either estimator at $\alpha=0$,
\[
\E\|\nabla_\theta\hat\ell_J^0-\nabla_\theta\ell^0\|_2^2
\lesssim pG'(\theta)^2\left(\frac NJ+\frac{N^2}{J^2}\right).
\]
The difference between the population score $\nabla_\theta\ell^0$ and
the full likelihood score is treated separately in the next section.}

\section{Proof of Theorem~{\thmMopBiasvar} (Bias-Variance Analysis)}
\label{appendix:sharper-biasvar}

\kevin{\normalfont\normalsize
Except where we explicitly average over observations, the data are fixed
and expectations are over particle-filter randomness.
At $\theta=\phi$, add and subtract the population discounted score:
\begin{align}
&\E\|\nabla_\theta\hat\ell^\alpha-\nabla_\theta\ell\|_2^2\notag\\
&\quad\leq
2\E\|\nabla_\theta\hat\ell^\alpha-\nabla_\theta\ell^\alpha\|_2^2
+2\|\nabla_\theta\ell^\alpha-\nabla_\theta\ell\|_2^2.
\label{eq:generic-mse-particle-population-split}
\end{align}
The first term is particle error and the second is population discounting
error. We first bound the population term for fixed observations and
under the data-generating law, then bound the particle term.
The reference parameter $\phi$ is held fixed during differentiation.
More explicitly, our notation for the population score means
\[
\nabla_\theta\ell_n^\alpha(\phi)
:=\left.\partial_\theta\log L_n^\alpha(\theta;\phi)
\right|_{\theta=\phi}.
\]
This is the derivative computed by the stop-gradient construction.
Differentiating along the diagonal $L_n^\alpha(\theta;\theta)$ would
also differentiate the reference law and would give the ordinary likelihood
score, since $L_n^\alpha(\theta;\theta)=p_\theta(y_n^*\mid y_{1:n-1}^*)$.
We derive the population score from the same weight recursion as the
particle score. Along each selected lineage,
\begin{equation}
\left.\nabla_\theta\log w_{n,j}^{F,\theta}\right|_{\theta=\phi}
=
\alpha
\left.\nabla_\theta\log w_{n-1,k_j}^{F,\theta}\right|_{\theta=\phi}
+
\left.\nabla_\theta\log g_{n,k_j}^{\theta}
\right|_{\theta=\phi}.
\label{eq:location-weight-gradient-pair-recursion}
\end{equation}
The last term is the measurement gradient $h_n$ defined in
Equation~(\ref{eq:actual-path-score}). This follows by differentiating
\begin{align}
\log w_{n,j}^{P,\theta}
&=\alpha\log w_{n-1,j}^{F,\theta},                         \notag\\
\log w_{n,j}^{F,\theta}
&=\alpha\log w_{n-1,k_j}^{F,\theta}
+\log g_{n,k_j}^{\theta}-\log g_{n,k_j}^{\phi}.
\label{eq:log-weight-recursion}
\end{align}
at $\theta=\phi$.
\endgraf
\paragraph*{Score identities.}
Let $U=(U_0,\ldots,U_N)$ contain the independent, parameter-free
simulator draws, including initialization, with joint law $\mu$.
Write $X_s^\theta=X_s^\theta(U_{0:s})$ and
$g_s^\theta(U)=f_{Y_s\mid X_s}(y_s^*\mid X_s^\theta(U_{0:s});\theta)$.
Thus the likelihood is
\[
p_\theta(y_{1:t}^*)=\int\prod_{s=1}^t g_s^\theta(u)\,\mu(du).
\]
Differentiation holds $U$ and the reference resampling indices fixed.
It includes the dependence of each state on $\theta$. The
measurement gradient on a simulator history satisfies
\begin{align}
h_k&=\left.\nabla_\theta\log g_k^{A,F,\theta}\right|_{\theta=\phi},
\label{eq:actual-path-score}\\
\|h_k\|_\infty&\leq G'(\theta).
\label{eq:bounded-realized-measurement-score}
\end{align}
For fixed $t$, the
product rule bounds every coordinate of the differentiated likelihood by
\begin{equation}
\left\|\nabla_\theta\prod_{s=1}^tG_s(X_s)\right\|_\infty
\leq \prod_{s=1}^tG_s(X_s)\sum_{s=1}^t
\|\nabla_\theta\log G_s(X_s)\|_\infty
\leq t\bar G^tG'(\theta).
\label{eq:finite-horizon-product-derivative-bound}
\end{equation}
The same local bound controls difference quotients by the mean-value
theorem, so dominated convergence gives
\begin{equation}
\nabla_\theta\E_U\left[\prod_{s=1}^tG_s(X_s)\right]
=\E_U\left[\nabla_\theta\prod_{s=1}^tG_s(X_s)\right].
\label{eq:finite-horizon-differentiation-under-expectation}
\end{equation}
Here $\E_U$ integrates $\mu$. On the reference pass, propagation appends
a fresh simulator draw and selection weights each history by $g_t^\phi$.
Starting from $\mu$, successive selections therefore give the population
history law
\begin{equation}
\rho_t^\phi(du)=
\frac{\prod_{s=1}^t g_s^\phi(u)\,\mu(du)}
{\int\prod_{s=1}^t g_s^\phi(v)\,\mu(dv)},
\qquad \rho_t=\rho_t^\phi.
\label{eq:canonical-extended-posterior-path-law}
\end{equation}
This is Bayes' formula for the simulator draws given $y_{1:t}^*$ under
$\phi$. The state and its parameter derivative are both functions of
these draws, so this law also specifies the derivative history.
Its current-state marginal is $\pi_t$. Finite particle histories
approximate $\rho_t^\phi$; their empirical means need not be unbiased.
Let
\begin{equation}
m(\xi_t)=\frac1J\sum_{j=1}^J\delta_{\xi_{t,j}},
\qquad m(\xi_t)(h_k)=\frac1J\sum_{j=1}^Jh_k(\xi_{t,j}),
\quad 1\leq k\leq t,
\label{eq:explicit-path-empirical-measure}
\end{equation}
where $\xi_{t,j}$ is the simulator history along particle $j$'s ancestry.
Iterating the filtered-weight recursion gives
\begin{equation}
\left.\nabla_\theta\log w_{n,j}^{F,\theta}\right|_{\theta=\phi}
=\sum_{r=0}^{n-1}\alpha^r h_{n-r}(\xi_{n,j}).
\label{eq:unrolled-filtered-weight}
\end{equation}
The prediction step preserves $j$, whereas selection enters only in the
filtered weight:
\begin{equation}
w_{n,j}^{P,\theta}=(w_{n-1,j}^{F,\theta})^\alpha,
\qquad w_{n,j}^{F,\theta}=w_{n,k_j}^{P,\theta}
\frac{g_{n,k_j}^{\theta}}{g_{n,k_j}^{\phi}}.
\label{eq:filtered-weight-current-resampling-index}
\end{equation}
The after-resampling likelihood increment is
\[
\widehat L_n^{A,\alpha}(\theta;\phi)
=\widehat L_n^\phi
\frac{J^{-1}\sum_jw_{n,j}^{F,\theta}}
     {J^{-1}\sum_jw_{n,j}^{P,\theta}}.
\]
The factor $\widehat L_n^\phi$ is frozen during differentiation, and
both sets of weights equal one at $\theta=\phi$. Differentiating the
log numerator and log denominator therefore yields
\begin{align}
\nabla_\theta\hat\ell_n^\alpha(\theta)
&=\frac1J\sum_j\left.\nabla_\theta\log w_{n,j}^{F,\theta}\right|_{\theta=\phi}
-\frac\alpha J\sum_j\left.\nabla_\theta\log w_{n-1,j}^{F,\theta}\right|_{\theta=\phi}
\label{eq:conditional-score-weight-difference}\\
&=m(\xi_n)(h_n)+\sum_{r=1}^{n-1}\alpha^r
\{m(\xi_n)(h_{n-r})-m(\xi_{n-1})(h_{n-r})\}.
\label{eq:conditional-score-path-decomposition}
\end{align}
Define the particle and population lag coefficients by
\begin{align}
\Delta_{n,0}^J&=m(\xi_n)(h_n),&\Delta_{n,0}&=\rho_n(h_n),\notag\\
\Delta_{n,r}^J&=m(\xi_n)(h_{n-r})-m(\xi_{n-1})(h_{n-r}),&
\Delta_{n,r}&=\rho_n(h_{n-r})-\rho_{n-1}(h_{n-r}),
\quad 1\leq r<n.
\label{eq:lag-coefficient-definitions}
\end{align}
For the population calculation, keep $\phi$ fixed and apply the weight
recursion to one simulator history:
\[
w_n^{F,\theta}(u)
=\prod_{s=1}^n\left\{\frac{g_s^\theta(u)}{g_s^\phi(u)}\right\}^{\alpha^{n-s}},
\qquad
w_n^{P,\theta}=(w_{n-1}^{F,\theta})^\alpha.
\]
The reference law satisfies
$\rho_n^\phi(q)=\rho_{n-1}^\phi(g_n^\phi q)/L_n^\phi$, where
$L_n^\phi=\rho_{n-1}^\phi(g_n^\phi)$ and the unused future draws retain
their original law. Since $w_n^{F,\theta}=w_n^{P,\theta}g_n^\theta/g_n^\phi$,
the before- and after-resampling population increments agree:
\[
L_n^\alpha(\theta;\phi)
=\frac{\rho_{n-1}^\phi(g_n^\theta w_n^{P,\theta})}
       {\rho_{n-1}^\phi(w_n^{P,\theta})}
=L_n^\phi\frac{\rho_n^\phi(w_n^{F,\theta})}
                  {\rho_{n-1}^\phi(w_n^{P,\theta})}.
\]
All expectations here are with respect to the fixed reference law.
At $\theta=\phi$, the weights equal one and their derivatives are
\[
\left.\partial_\theta w_n^{F,\theta}\right|_{\theta=\phi}
=\sum_{s=1}^n\alpha^{n-s}h_s,
\qquad
\left.\partial_\theta w_n^{P,\theta}\right|_{\theta=\phi}
=\sum_{s=1}^{n-1}\alpha^{n-s}h_s.
\]
The local bounds used above also justify differentiating these fixed-horizon
weight integrals: each ratio $g_s^\theta/g_s^\phi$ and its derivative is
bounded on the parameter neighborhood. Consequently,
\[
\left.\partial_\theta\log L_n^\alpha(\theta;\phi)\right|_{\theta=\phi}
=\rho_n\left(\sum_{s=1}^n\alpha^{n-s}h_s\right)
-\rho_{n-1}\left(\sum_{s=1}^{n-1}\alpha^{n-s}h_s\right).
\]
The second term comes from the prediction-weight denominator.
\endgraf
For comparison, differentiating the likelihood integral gives
\begin{equation}
\left.\partial_\theta\log p_\theta(y_{1:n}^*)\right|_{\theta=\phi}
=\frac{\int\!\left(\sum_{s=1}^n h_s(u)\right)
                 \prod_{s=1}^n g_s^\phi(u)\,\mu(du)}
       {\int\prod_{s=1}^n g_s^\phi(u)\,\mu(du)}
=\sum_{s=1}^n\rho_n(h_s).
\label{eq:full-population-score-path-average}
\end{equation}
This is Fisher's identity applied to the simulator draws; the usual
state-trajectory version is given by \citet[Equation~(4)]{poyiadjis11}.
Here process and initialization derivatives enter through $X_s^\theta(U)$,
while $\mu$ is parameter-free. Subtracting the time-$(n-1)$ likelihood
score, and collecting the terms in the discounted derivative, gives
\begin{align}
\nabla_\theta\ell_n(\theta)
&=\rho_n(h_n)+\sum_{r=1}^{n-1}
\{\rho_n(h_{n-r})-\rho_{n-1}(h_{n-r})\}
=\sum_{r=0}^{n-1}\Delta_{n,r},
\label{eq:exact-population-conditional-score-path-decomposition}\\
\nabla_\theta\ell_n^\alpha(\theta)
&=\rho_n(h_n)+\sum_{r=1}^{n-1}\alpha^r
\{\rho_n(h_{n-r})-\rho_{n-1}(h_{n-r})\}
=\sum_{r=0}^{n-1}\alpha^r\Delta_{n,r}.
\label{eq:population-conditional-score-path-decomposition}
\end{align}
\endgraf
}

\kevin{\normalfont\normalsize
\begin{lem}[Full-score summation by parts]
\label{lem:dmop-full-score-summation-by-parts}
For $\theta=\phi$ and $\alpha\in[0,1]$, the after-resampling score and
its population counterpart satisfy
\begin{align}
\nabla_\theta\hat\ell^\alpha
&=(1-\alpha)\sum_{t=1}^{N-1}
 m(\xi_t)\left(\sum_{k=1}^t\alpha^{t-k}h_k\right)
 +m(\xi_N)\left(\sum_{k=1}^N\alpha^{N-k}h_k\right),
\label{eq:full-score-summation-by-parts}\\
\nabla_\theta\ell^\alpha
&=(1-\alpha)\sum_{t=1}^{N-1}
 \rho_t\left(\sum_{k=1}^t\alpha^{t-k}h_k\right)
 +\rho_N\left(\sum_{k=1}^N\alpha^{N-k}h_k\right).
\label{eq:population-full-score-summation-by-parts}
\end{align}
For every coordinate $i$ and $t\leq N$,
\begin{equation}
\left\|\sum_{k=1}^t\alpha^{t-k}h_{k,i}\right\|_\infty
\leq G'(\theta)\sum_{k=1}^t\alpha^{t-k}.
\label{eq:discounted-score-supremum}
\end{equation}
\end{lem}
\begin{proof}
Summing Equation~(\ref{eq:conditional-score-path-decomposition}) by
creation time gives
\begin{equation}
\nabla_\theta\hat\ell^\alpha
=\sum_{k=1}^N\left[m(\xi_k)(h_k)
+\sum_{t=k+1}^N\alpha^{t-k}
 \{m(\xi_t)(h_k)-m(\xi_{t-1})(h_k)\}\right].
\label{eq:full-score-reindexed-by-contribution}
\end{equation}
For each $k$, collect the coefficient at every endpoint:
\begin{align}
&m(\xi_k)(h_k)+\sum_{t=k+1}^N\alpha^{t-k}
 \{m(\xi_t)(h_k)-m(\xi_{t-1})(h_k)\}\notag\\
&\quad=(1-\alpha)\sum_{t=k}^{N-1}\alpha^{t-k}m(\xi_t)(h_k)
 +\alpha^{N-k}m(\xi_N)(h_k).
\label{eq:one-score-summation-by-parts}
\end{align}
Interchanging the finite sums proves the particle identity. Replacing
$m(\xi_t)$ by $\rho_t$ proves the population identity. The coordinate
bound follows from $\|h_{k,i}\|_\infty\leq G'(\theta)$.
\end{proof}
}

\kevin{\normalfont\normalsize
\begin{lem}[Lagwise first-derivative forgetting]
\label{lem:derivative-measure-forgetting}
Suppose $\theta=\phi$. Under Assumptions {\refAssumpBoundedProcess},
{\refAssumpBoundedMeasurement}, and {\refAssumpDifferentiableSimulator},
there are constants $C<\infty$ and $\varrho\in(0,1)$ such that
\begin{equation}
\|\Delta_{n,r}\|_2\leq C\sqrt p\,G'(\theta)\varrho^r,
\qquad 0\leq r<n.
\label{eq:derivative-measure-forgetting}
\end{equation}
\end{lem}
\endgraf
\begin{proof}[Proof.]
Fix $1\leq r<n$, put $k=n-r$, and consider coordinate $i$.
We first integrate out the history in $h_{k,i}$. Set
\begin{align}
\bar h_{k,i}(X_k)&=\E_{\rho_k}(h_{k,i}\mid X_k),
\label{eq:population-score-current-projection}\\
L_{k,t}(X_k)&=\E_U\left[\left.\prod_{s=k+1}^tG_s(X_s)\right|X_k\right],
\qquad L_{k,k}=1.
\label{eq:population-score-future-likelihood}
\end{align}
Future simulator draws are independent of the history given $X_k$.
Thus, for every bounded $q$ and $t\geq k$,
\begin{equation}
\rho_t\{h_{k,i}q(X_k)\}
=\frac{\rho_k\{h_{k,i}q(X_k)L_{k,t}(X_k)\}}{\rho_k(L_{k,t})}
=\frac{\rho_k\{\bar h_{k,i}q(X_k)L_{k,t}(X_k)\}}{\rho_k(L_{k,t})}.
\label{eq:population-score-projection-invariance}
\end{equation}
Consequently $\E_{\rho_t}(h_{k,i}\mid X_k)=\bar h_{k,i}(X_k)$.
This conditional independence also holds given a later state, so the
physical backward kernels propagate the projected historical score.
They are
\begin{equation}
B_s(x_s,dx_{s-1})=
\frac{\pi_{s-1}(dx_{s-1})
f_{X_s\mid X_{s-1}}(x_s\mid x_{s-1};\theta)}
{\int\pi_{s-1}(du)f_{X_s\mid X_{s-1}}(x_s\mid u;\theta)}.
\label{eq:posterior-backward-kernel}
\end{equation}
With $\epsilon=\underbar M/\bar M$, the transition bounds imply
\begin{equation}
B_s(x_s,\cdot)\geq\epsilon\pi_{s-1}(\cdot),
\qquad \operatorname{osc}(B_sf)\leq(1-\epsilon)\operatorname{osc}(f).
\label{eq:posterior-backward-oscillation-contraction}
\end{equation}
Indeed, subtracting the common mass $\epsilon\pi_{s-1}$ leaves
$(1-\epsilon)$ times a probability kernel. It follows that
\begin{align}
H(x)&:=\E_{\rho_{n-1}}(h_{k,i}\mid X_{n-1}=x)
=(B_{n-1}\cdots B_{k+1}\bar h_{k,i})(x),
\label{eq:iterated-backward-conditioning}\\
\operatorname{osc}(H)&\leq2G'(\theta)(1-\epsilon)^{r-1}.
\label{eq:conditional-past-score-forgetting}
\end{align}
An empty kernel product is the identity, and a zeroth power here is one,
including when $\epsilon=1$.
\endgraf
Integrating the last simulator draw gives the posterior update
\begin{equation}
\rho_n(h_{k,i})=
\frac{\rho_{n-1}\{h_{k,i}M_n(G_n)\}}{\rho_{n-1}M_n(G_n)},
\qquad M_n(G_n)(x)=\int M_n(x,du)G_n(u).
\label{eq:posterior-path-update-functional}
\end{equation}
Writing the numerator using $H$ therefore gives
\begin{equation}
\Delta_{n,r,i}=
\frac{\pi_{n-1}[(H-\pi_{n-1}H)
\{M_n(G_n)-\pi_{n-1}M_n(G_n)\}]}{\pi_{n-1}M_n(G_n)}.
\label{eq:lag-coefficient-covariance-identity}
\end{equation}
The denominator is at least $\underbar G$. Also,
$M_n(x,\cdot)\geq\epsilon M_n(x^\star,\cdot)$ for any fixed
$x^\star$, so subtracting this common mass shows that
\begin{equation}
\operatorname{osc}\{M_n(G_n)\}
\leq(1-\epsilon)(\bar G-\underbar G).
\label{eq:predictive-potential-oscillation}
\end{equation}
Combining the last three displays with
$|f-\pi_{n-1}f|\leq\operatorname{osc}(f)$ gives
\begin{equation}
|\Delta_{n,r,i}|\leq
\frac{2(\bar G-\underbar G)}{\underbar G}
G'(\theta)(1-\epsilon)^r.
\label{eq:lag-coordinate-geometric-bound}
\end{equation}
Choose
\begin{equation}
\varrho=1-\frac{\underbar M}{2\bar M}\in(0,1).
\label{eq:population-forgetting-varrho}
\end{equation}
Since $1-\epsilon\leq\varrho$, summing squared coordinate bounds gives
the result for $r\geq1$. For $r=0$,
$\|\rho_n(h_n)\|_2\leq\sqrt p\,G'(\theta)$.
If $\epsilon=1$, $M_n(G_n)$ is constant and every positive-lag
coefficient is zero.
\end{proof}
\endgraf
\endgraf
}

\kevin{\normalfont\normalsize
\begin{lem}[Population discounting bias]
\label{lem:dmop-population-discount-bias}
Under the conditions of Lemma~\ref{lem:derivative-measure-forgetting},
suppose $\theta=\phi$ and $\alpha\in[0,1]$. Then
\begin{align}
\left\|
\nabla_\theta\ell_n^\alpha(\theta)
-\nabla_\theta\ell_n(\theta)
\right\|_2
&\leq
C\sqrt p\,G'(\theta)
\sum_{r=1}^{n-1}(1-\alpha^r)\varrho^r                       \notag\\
&\leq
C\sqrt p\,G'(\theta)
\frac{\varrho(1-\alpha)}
{(1-\varrho)(1-\alpha\varrho)}.
\label{eq:dmop-population-discount-bias}
\end{align}
For the full score,
\begin{align}
&\left\|
\nabla_\theta\ell(\theta)
-\nabla_\theta\ell^\alpha(\theta)
\right\|_2                                                     \notag\\
&\quad\leq
C\sqrt p\,G'(\theta)\varrho(1-\alpha)
\sum_{q=0}^{N-2}(N-1-q)(\alpha\varrho)^q                    \notag\\
&\quad=
C\sqrt p\,G'(\theta)\varrho(1-\alpha)
\frac{(N-1)-N\alpha\varrho+(\alpha\varrho)^N}
{(1-\alpha\varrho)^2}                                       \notag\\
&\quad\leq
C(N-1)\sqrt p\,G'(\theta)
\frac{\varrho(1-\alpha)}{1-\alpha\varrho}.
\label{eq:dmop-full-population-discount-bias}
\end{align}
For $N=1$, the finite sum is empty, the displayed fraction is zero, and
the full-score difference is zero.
The dependence on the transition bounds can be sharpened further.
With the existing $\varrho=1-\underbar M/(2\bar M)$ from Equation~
(\ref{eq:population-forgetting-varrho}), for $\tfrac12<\varrho<1$,
\begin{align}
&\left\|\nabla_\theta\ell(\theta)
-\nabla_\theta\ell^\alpha(\theta)\right\|_2                    \notag\\
&\quad\leq
2\sqrt p\,G'(\theta)
\frac{1-\sqrt{2(1-\varrho)}}{1+\sqrt{2(1-\varrho)}}(1-\alpha)
\sum_{q=0}^{N-2}(N-1-q)
\left\{\alpha\frac{2\varrho-1}{3-2\varrho}\right\}^{q}       \notag\\
&\quad\leq
2(N-1)\sqrt p\,G'(\theta)
\frac{1-\sqrt{2(1-\varrho)}}{1+\sqrt{2(1-\varrho)}}
\frac{(1-\alpha)(3-2\varrho)}
{3-2\varrho-\alpha(2\varrho-1)}.
\label{eq:population-discount-sharp-transition-bound}
\end{align}
If every transition is independent of its starting location, in
particular if $\varrho=\tfrac12$, the population discounting bias is zero
for every $N$ and $\alpha$.
\end{lem}
\endgraf
\begin{proof}[Proof.]
The score identities give the exact difference
\begin{equation}
\nabla_\theta\ell_n(\theta)-\nabla_\theta\ell_n^\alpha(\theta)
=\sum_{r=1}^{n-1}(1-\alpha^r)\Delta_{n,r}.
\label{eq:population-discount-lag-difference}
\end{equation}
In particular, $\nabla_\theta\ell_n^0(\theta)=\Delta_{n,0}$, whereas
$\alpha=1$ gives the exact score. Lemma~\ref{lem:derivative-measure-forgetting}
and the geometric-series identity
\begin{equation}
\sum_{r=1}^\infty(1-\alpha^r)\varrho^r
=\frac{\varrho}{1-\varrho}-\frac{\alpha\varrho}{1-\alpha\varrho}
=\frac{\varrho(1-\alpha)}{(1-\varrho)(1-\alpha\varrho)}
\end{equation}
prove the conditional-score bound.
\endgraf
For the full score, summation by parts retains the cancellation across
observation times. The contribution of $h_s$ to the discounted score is
\begin{align}
\rho_s(h_s)+\sum_{q=1}^{N-s}\alpha^q
\{\rho_{s+q}(h_s)-\rho_{s+q-1}(h_s)\}
&=(1-\alpha)\sum_{q=0}^{N-s-1}\alpha^q\rho_{s+q}(h_s)
+\alpha^{N-s}\rho_N(h_s).
\label{eq:population-discount-summation-by-parts}
\end{align}
Subtract this from $\rho_N(h_s)$ and sum over $s$ to obtain
\begin{equation}
\nabla_\theta\ell(\theta)-\nabla_\theta\ell^\alpha(\theta)
=(1-\alpha)\sum_{s=1}^{N-1}\sum_{q=0}^{N-s-1}\alpha^q
\{\rho_N(h_s)-\rho_{s+q}(h_s)\}.
\label{eq:population-discount-full-boundary-comparison}
\end{equation}
We bound each comparison directly, using a likelihood-ratio inequality.
For a probability measure $\mu$ and $0<a\leq L\leq b$, put $m=\mu L$.
When $a<b$, the chord bound for the convex function $|L-m|$ gives
\begin{equation}
\mu|L-m|\leq\frac{2(b-m)(m-a)}{b-a}.
\label{eq:population-discount-likelihood-chord}
\end{equation}
Subtracting the midpoint of the range of $f$ from
$\mu[f(L-m)]$ therefore yields
\begin{align}
\left|\frac{\mu(fL)}{\mu L}-\mu f\right|
&\leq\frac{\operatorname{osc}(f)}{2m}\mu|L-m|\notag\\
&\leq\operatorname{osc}(f)\frac{a+b-m-ab/m}{b-a}
\leq\operatorname{osc}(f)\frac{\sqrt b-\sqrt a}{\sqrt b+\sqrt a}.
\label{eq:population-discount-bayes-range-bound}
\end{align}
The last inequality uses $m+ab/m\geq2\sqrt{ab}$. For $a=b$, both
sides are zero.
\endgraf
Write $\epsilon=\underbar M/\bar M=2(1-\varrho)$ and, for this proof,
\begin{equation}
\beta=\frac{1-\epsilon}{1+\epsilon}
=\frac{2\varrho-1}{3-2\varrho},
\qquad
\gamma=\frac{1-\sqrt\epsilon}{1+\sqrt\epsilon}
=\frac{1-\sqrt{2(1-\varrho)}}{1+\sqrt{2(1-\varrho)}}.
\end{equation}
For two backward kernels $B_t(x,\cdot)$ and $B_t(x',\cdot)$,
the former is a likelihood tilt of the latter by
\begin{equation}
L(u)=\frac{f_{X_t\mid X_{t-1}}(x\mid u;\theta)}
{f_{X_t\mid X_{t-1}}(x'\mid u;\theta)},
\qquad \epsilon\leq L(u)\leq\epsilon^{-1}.
\end{equation}
Equation~(\ref{eq:population-discount-bayes-range-bound}), with
$a=\epsilon$ and $b=\epsilon^{-1}$, gives
\begin{equation}
\operatorname{osc}(B_tf)\leq\beta\operatorname{osc}(f).
\label{eq:population-discount-sharp-backward-contraction}
\end{equation}
For $s\leq t=s+q<N$, future observations are conditionally independent
of the extended history through $t$ given $X_t$. The projection argument
in Lemma~\ref{lem:derivative-measure-forgetting} thus gives
\begin{equation}
H_{s,t,i}(x):=\E_{\rho_N}(h_{s,i}\mid X_t=x)
=\E_{\rho_t}(h_{s,i}\mid X_t=x)
=(B_t\cdots B_{s+1}\bar h_{s,i})(x).
\label{eq:population-discount-common-conditional-past}
\end{equation}
The kernel product is empty if $q=0$. Since
$\operatorname{osc}(\bar h_{s,i})\leq2G'(\theta)$,
\begin{equation}
\operatorname{osc}(H_{s,t,i})\leq2G'(\theta)\beta^q.
\label{eq:population-discount-partial-smoother-oscillation}
\end{equation}
The future likelihood in Equation~(\ref{eq:population-score-future-likelihood})
satisfies
\begin{equation}
L_{t,N}(x)=\int M_{t+1}(x,du)G_{t+1}(u)L_{t+1,N}(u),
\qquad \epsilon\leq\frac{L_{t,N}(x)}{L_{t,N}(x')}\leq\epsilon^{-1}.
\label{eq:population-discount-future-likelihood-ratio}
\end{equation}
The ratio bound follows from the transition bounds on the first kernel.
The two expectations being compared are consequently
\begin{equation}
\rho_N(h_{s,i})=\frac{\pi_t(H_{s,t,i}L_{t,N})}{\pi_t(L_{t,N})},
\qquad \rho_t(h_{s,i})=\pi_t(H_{s,t,i}).
\label{eq:population-discount-two-boundary-expectations}
\end{equation}
Here $\sup L_{t,N}/\inf L_{t,N}\leq\epsilon^{-1}$, so the same
likelihood-tilt inequality bounds their difference by
$\gamma\operatorname{osc}(H_{s,t,i})$. Summing squared coordinate bounds
gives
\begin{align}
\|\rho_N(h_s)-\rho_{s+q}(h_s)\|_2
&\leq2\sqrt p\,G'(\theta)\gamma\beta^q\notag\\
&=2\sqrt p\,G'(\theta)
\frac{1-\sqrt{2(1-\varrho)}}{1+\sqrt{2(1-\varrho)}}
\left(\frac{2\varrho-1}{3-2\varrho}\right)^q
\leq2\sqrt p\,G'(\theta)\varrho^{q+1},
\label{eq:population-discount-vector-boundary-comparison}
\end{align}
since $\beta,\gamma\leq1-\epsilon=2\varrho-1\leq\varrho$.
If transitions are independent of their starting location, $L_{t,N}$
is constant and these differences vanish. This includes $\epsilon=1$.
\endgraf
Insert the sharper comparison into
Equation~(\ref{eq:population-discount-full-boundary-comparison}) and
reverse the finite sums:
\begin{align}
\|\nabla_\theta\ell-\nabla_\theta\ell^\alpha\|_2
&\leq2\sqrt p\,G'(\theta)\gamma(1-\alpha)
\sum_{q=0}^{N-2}(N-1-q)(\alpha\beta)^q\notag\\
&\leq2(N-1)\sqrt p\,G'(\theta)
\frac{\gamma(1-\alpha)}{1-\alpha\beta}.
\end{align}
Substituting $\beta$ and $\gamma$ proves
Equation~(\ref{eq:population-discount-sharp-transition-bound}). Using
$\gamma\beta^q\leq\varrho^{q+1}$ instead gives the full-score bound,
with the finite sum evaluated by
\begin{align}
(1-z)\sum_{q=0}^{N-2}(N-1-q)z^q
&=(N-1)-\sum_{q=1}^{N-1}z^q,\notag\\
\sum_{q=0}^{N-2}(N-1-q)z^q
&=\frac{(N-1)-Nz+z^N}{(1-z)^2},\qquad z=\alpha\varrho<1.
\label{eq:population-discount-finite-boundary-sum}
\end{align}
For $N=1$ all comparisons are empty. The factor $1-\alpha$ handles
$\alpha=1$, and the formulas at $\alpha=0$ retain only $q=0$.
\end{proof}
\endgraf
\begin{remark}[Signed cancellation in the population bias]
\label{rem:population-bias-cancellation}
Retaining signs gives a first-order expansion of the population bias.
The score is a finite polynomial in $\alpha$, so
\begin{align}
\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial\alpha}
\nabla_\theta\ell^\alpha(\theta)\right|_{\alpha=1}
&=\sum_{n=1}^{N}\sum_{r=1}^{n-1}r\Delta_{n,r}                \notag\\
&=\sum_{s=1}^{N-1}\sum_{q=0}^{N-s-1}
\{\rho_N(h_s)-\rho_{s+q}(h_s)\}.
\label{eq:population-bias-signed-derivative}
\end{align}
Differentiating the finite lag sum gives the first equality, and
Equation~(\ref{eq:population-discount-full-boundary-comparison}) gives
the second. Subtracting this first-order term yields
\begin{align}
&\nabla_\theta\ell(\theta)-\nabla_\theta\ell^\alpha(\theta)
-(1-\alpha)
\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial\alpha}
\nabla_\theta\ell^\alpha(\theta)\right|_{\alpha=1}             \notag\\
&\quad=-(1-\alpha)\sum_{s=1}^{N-1}\sum_{q=0}^{N-s-1}
(1-\alpha^q)\{\rho_N(h_s)-\rho_{s+q}(h_s)\}.
\label{eq:population-bias-signed-remainder}
\end{align}
By Equation~(\ref{eq:population-discount-vector-boundary-comparison}),
for $\tfrac12<\varrho<1$,
\begin{align}
&\left\|
\nabla_\theta\ell(\theta)-\nabla_\theta\ell^\alpha(\theta)
-(1-\alpha)
\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial\alpha}
\nabla_\theta\ell^\alpha(\theta)\right|_{\alpha=1}
\right\|_2                                                   \notag\\*
&\quad\leq
2\sqrt p\,G'(\theta)\frac{1-\sqrt{2(1-\varrho)}}{1+\sqrt{2(1-\varrho)}}
(1-\alpha)\sum_{q=0}^{N-2}(N-1-q)(1-\alpha^q)
\left(\frac{2\varrho-1}{3-2\varrho}\right)^q                \notag\\
&\quad\leq
2(N-1)\sqrt p\,G'(\theta)\frac{1-\sqrt{2(1-\varrho)}}{1+\sqrt{2(1-\varrho)}}
(1-\alpha)
\left\{\frac{3-2\varrho}{4(1-\varrho)}
-\frac{3-2\varrho}{3-2\varrho-\alpha(2\varrho-1)}\right\}     \notag\\
&\quad=
(N-1)\sqrt p\,G'(\theta)
\frac{(1-\sqrt{2(1-\varrho)})^2(3-2\varrho)(1-\alpha)^2}
{2(1-\varrho)\{3-2\varrho-\alpha(2\varrho-1)\}}.
\label{eq:population-bias-signed-remainder-bound}
\end{align}
The last equality uses
$2\varrho-1=(1-\sqrt{2(1-\varrho)})(1+\sqrt{2(1-\varrho)})$.
For $\varrho=\tfrac12$, the score difference and its signed derivative are
both zero, so the same bound holds.
In particular, the population bias is bounded by
\begin{align}
&\|\nabla_\theta\ell(\theta)
-\nabla_\theta\ell^\alpha(\theta)\|_2                         \notag\\
&\quad\leq
(1-\alpha)
\left\|\sum_{n=1}^{N}\sum_{r=1}^{n-1}r\Delta_{n,r}\right\|_2
+
(N-1)\sqrt p\,G'(\theta)
\frac{(1-\sqrt{2(1-\varrho)})^2(3-2\varrho)(1-\alpha)^2}
{2(1-\varrho)\{3-2\varrho-\alpha(2\varrho-1)\}}.
\label{eq:population-bias-cancellation-bound}
\end{align}
For every fixed observation sequence, if the signed sum vanishes, this
gives $O\{N(1-\alpha)^2\}$ bias and $O\{N^2(1-\alpha)^4\}$ squared bias,
with fixed model constants.
\end{remark}
\endgraf
}
\endgraf

\subsection{Population error averaged over observations}
\label{sec:population-data-average}
\kevin{\normalfont\normalsize
The preceding bounds hold for each fixed observation sequence. We now
generate $Y_{1:N}$ from the POMP at $\theta_0$ and evaluate both scores at
$\theta=\phi=\theta_0$. The expectation $\E_{\theta_0}$ averages over
these data. It does not average over particle-filter randomness.
The posterior law in Equation~(\ref{eq:canonical-extended-posterior-path-law})
then satisfies
\begin{equation}
\rho_n(h_s)=\E_{\theta_0}(h_s\mid\mathcal F_n^Y),
\qquad \mathcal F_n^Y=\sigma(Y_1,\ldots,Y_n),\qquad s\leq n.
\label{eq:data-average-posterior-law}
\end{equation}
Here $h_s$ is the full pathwise measurement derivative in
Equation~(\ref{eq:actual-path-score}), evaluated with the observation
held fixed. The causal simulator uses fresh, independent,
parameter-free draws at each time, including initialization.}

\begin{prop}[Population error under the data-generating law]
\label{prop:dmop-alpha-data-averaged-score-discrepancy}
\kevin{\normalfont\normalsize
Suppose (A2), (A3), and (A5) hold in a neighborhood of $\theta_0$, with
the same deterministic bounds over the support of the observations.
In particular, let $G'(\theta_0)$ bound the full pathwise measurement
derivatives throughout this neighborhood. Fix $\alpha\in[0,1]$
independently of the observations, and evaluate at
$\theta=\phi=\theta_0$. For either population score,
\begin{align}
&\E_{\theta_0}\left\|
\nabla_\theta\ell^\alpha(\theta_0)-\nabla_\theta\ell(\theta_0)
\right\|_2^2 \notag\\
&\quad\leq C(N-1)pG'(\theta_0)^2
\left\{\frac{\varrho(1-\alpha)}
{(1-\varrho)(1-\alpha\varrho)}\right\}^{2}
\lesssim NpG'(\theta_0)^2(1-\alpha)^2,
\label{eq:data-averaged-population-score-bound}
\end{align}
where $\varrho$ is the forgetting constant of
Lemma~\ref{lem:derivative-measure-forgetting} and the constants are
independent of $N$ and $\alpha$. Neither (A6) nor (A6-J) is required.}
\end{prop}

\begin{proof}
\kevin{\normalfont\normalsize
Subtract the score identities in Equations~
(\ref{eq:exact-population-conditional-score-path-decomposition}) and
(\ref{eq:population-conditional-score-path-decomposition}) at the fixed
reference $\phi=\theta_0$. By Equation~(\ref{eq:data-average-posterior-law}),
\begin{align}
e_n^\alpha
&:=\nabla_\theta\ell_n(\theta_0)
   -\nabla_\theta\ell_n^\alpha(\theta_0)\notag\\*
&=\sum_{s=1}^{n-1}(1-\alpha^{n-s})
\left\{\E_{\theta_0}(h_s\mid\mathcal F_n^Y)
      -\E_{\theta_0}(h_s\mid\mathcal F_{n-1}^Y)\right\}.
\label{eq:data-averaged-score-increment}
\end{align}
The current contribution $\rho_n(h_n)$ cancels. For each $s<n$, both
conditional expectations concern the same random variable
$h_s(U_{0:s},Y_s)$, with $Y_s$ already observed at time $n-1$.
The tower property gives
\[
\E_{\theta_0}\!\left[
\E_{\theta_0}(h_s\mid\mathcal F_n^Y)
\mid\mathcal F_{n-1}^Y\right]
=\E_{\theta_0}(h_s\mid\mathcal F_{n-1}^Y).
\]
For completeness, if $Z=\E(h_s\mid\mathcal F_n^Y)$, then for every
$A\in\mathcal F_{n-1}^Y\subseteq\mathcal F_n^Y$,
$\E(\mathbf1_AZ)=\E(\mathbf1_Ah_s)$ by the definition of conditional
expectation. Conditioning $Z$ on $\mathcal F_{n-1}^Y$ therefore gives
$\E(h_s\mid\mathcal F_{n-1}^Y)$ by the same definition
\citep[pp.~1--3]{wolpert08conditional}. Since the coefficients
$1-\alpha^{n-s}$ are deterministic, it follows that
\[
\E_{\theta_0}(e_n^\alpha\mid\mathcal F_{n-1}^Y)=0.
\]
For $m<n$, $e_m^\alpha$ is $\mathcal F_{n-1}^Y$-measurable, so
\begin{align}
\E_{\theta_0}\{(e_m^\alpha)^{\mathsf T}e_n^\alpha\}
&=\E_{\theta_0}\left[
(e_m^\alpha)^{\mathsf T}
\E_{\theta_0}(e_n^\alpha\mid\mathcal F_{n-1}^Y)\right]=0,\notag\\
\E_{\theta_0}\left\|\sum_{n=1}^N e_n^\alpha\right\|_2^2
&=\sum_{n=1}^N\E_{\theta_0}\|e_n^\alpha\|_2^2.
\label{eq:data-averaged-score-orthogonality}
\end{align}
The observation-uniform version of
Lemma~\ref{lem:derivative-measure-forgetting} bounds each increment by
\begin{align}
\|e_n^\alpha\|_2
&\leq C\sqrt p\,G'(\theta_0)
       \sum_{r=1}^{n-1}(1-\alpha^r)\varrho^r\notag\\
&\leq C\sqrt p\,G'(\theta_0)
\left\{\frac{\varrho}{1-\varrho}
       -\frac{\alpha\varrho}{1-\alpha\varrho}\right\}
=C\sqrt p\,G'(\theta_0)
\frac{\varrho(1-\alpha)}{(1-\varrho)(1-\alpha\varrho)}.
\end{align}
Since $e_1^\alpha=0$, substitution into
Equation~(\ref{eq:data-averaged-score-orthogonality}) proves the result.}
\end{proof}

\kevin{\normalfont\normalsize
At a different evaluation parameter, the posterior increments need not
have conditional mean zero under the data-generating law. The following
bound separates their centered fluctuations from the resulting drift.}

\begin{prop}[Population error away from the generating parameter]
\label{prop:population-offtruth}
\kevin{\normalfont\normalsize
Suppose the assumptions of Proposition~
\ref{prop:dmop-alpha-data-averaged-score-discrepancy} hold uniformly on
a convex neighborhood $\mathcal V$ of $\theta_0$. Let $B$ bound every
coordinate of the full pathwise measurement derivative on this neighborhood,
and put
\[
b=1-\underbar M/\bar M,\qquad
K=\frac{2\sqrt p\,B}{(1-b)^2},\qquad
L=\sqrt p\,B\left(1+\frac{2}{1-b}\right).
\]
Fix $\vartheta\in\mathcal V$ and $\alpha\in[0,1]$ independently of the
data, and write $\delta=\|\vartheta-\theta_0\|_2$.
Evaluate the discounted population score with reference
$\phi=\vartheta$ held fixed during differentiation. Then
\begin{align}
&\left(\E_{\theta_0}\left\|
\nabla_\theta\ell(\vartheta)-\nabla_\theta\ell^\alpha(\vartheta)
\right\|_2^2\right)^{1/2}\notag\\*
&\qquad\leq K(1-\alpha)
\left\{\sqrt{N-1}+2L(N-1)\delta\right\}.
\label{eq:population-offtruth-root-mse}
\end{align}
In particular, with fixed model constants the squared error is
$O\{(1-\alpha)^2(N+N^2\delta^2)\}$. The explicit constants above retain
the additional dependence on $pB^2$ in the drift term.}
\end{prop}

\begin{proof}
\kevin{\normalfont\normalsize
Write $e_n^\alpha(\vartheta)$ for the exact minus discounted conditional
score. The score identities give
\[
e_n^\alpha(\vartheta)
=\sum_{s<n}(1-\alpha^{n-s})
\{\rho_n^\vartheta(h_s^\vartheta)
 -\rho_{n-1}^\vartheta(h_s^\vartheta)\}.
\]
For each past in the generating support, we construct a bounded extension
centered under the predictive law at $\vartheta$. We then bound its
conditional mean under the generating law at $\theta_0$.
\endgraf
Fix a past $y_{1:n-1}$ in the support of the generating law.
For each $s<n$, project its historical score onto $X_{n-1}$:
\[
H_{s,n-1}(x)=
\E_\vartheta(h_s^\vartheta\mid X_{n-1}=x,Y_{1:n-1}=y_{1:n-1}).
\]
The backward-kernel contraction in
Equation~(\ref{eq:posterior-backward-oscillation-contraction}) gives
\[
\sup_{x,x'}\|H_{s,n-1}(x)-H_{s,n-1}(x')\|_2
\leq2\sqrt p\,B\,b^{n-s-1}.
\]
The exponent is zero when $s=n-1$, with the empty kernel product equal
to the identity. Let $\pi$ be the law of $X_{n-1}$ given this past under $\vartheta$, and
write $R_y(x)=p_\vartheta(y\mid X_{n-1}=x)$. Bayes' formula gives
\[
\rho_n^{\vartheta,y}(h_s^\vartheta)
=\frac{\pi(H_{s,n-1}R_y)}{\pi(R_y)},
\qquad \rho_{n-1}^\vartheta(h_s^\vartheta)=\pi(H_{s,n-1}).
\]
Here the superscript $y$ specifies the new observation. The first
expectation reweights $\pi$ by the nonnegative function $R_y$, so the
difference of these two expectations is at most the displayed diameter.
Consequently, the historical-score
formula defines $e_n^\alpha(\vartheta;y)$ for every next observation
with positive predictive density under $\vartheta$, and
\begin{align}
\|e_n^\alpha(\vartheta;y)\|_2
&\leq2\sqrt p\,B\sum_{r=1}^{n-1}(1-\alpha^r)b^{r-1}\notag\\*
&\leq\frac{2\sqrt p\,B(1-\alpha)}{(1-b)(1-\alpha b)}
\leq K(1-\alpha).
\label{eq:offtruth-increment-bound}
\end{align}
Only historical measurement derivatives occur here. The bound therefore
also applies to candidate observations outside the generating support.
On that support the formula equals the score difference; elsewhere it
provides a bounded extension without requiring a derivative of the current
measurement density. If $\nu$ is the observation dominating measure,
then $p_\vartheta(y\mid y_{1:n-1})=\pi(R_y)$ and
$\int R_y(x)\,\nu(dy)=1$. Boundedness of $H_{s,n-1}$ therefore gives
\[
\int \rho_n^{\vartheta,y}(h_s^\vartheta)
 p_\vartheta(y\mid y_{1:n-1})\,\nu(dy)
=\pi\left(H_{s,n-1}\int R_y\,d\nu\right)
=\rho_{n-1}^\vartheta(h_s^\vartheta).
\]
Subtracting the last term and summing the historical coefficients gives
\[
\int e_n^\alpha(\vartheta;y)
 p_\vartheta(y\mid y_{1:n-1})\,\nu(dy)=0.
\]
\endgraf
For any $\psi\in\mathcal V$, the same backward bound applied to the
undiscounted conditional-score identity gives, on the generating support,
\begin{align}
\|\nabla_\psi\log p_\psi(y\mid y_{1:n-1})\|_2
&\leq\sqrt p\,B+2\sqrt p\,B\sum_{r=1}^{n-1}b^{r-1}
\leq L.
\label{eq:offtruth-predictive-score-bound}
\end{align}
The fixed-horizon differentiation argument above justifies this score
identity throughout $\mathcal V$. Integrating along the segment from
$\theta_0$ to $\vartheta$ gives
\[
p_\vartheta(y\mid y_{1:n-1})
\geq e^{-L\delta}p_{\theta_0}(y\mid y_{1:n-1}).
\]
For two densities $q,r$, their total variation distance is
$1-\int\min(q,r)\,d\nu$. Thus the predictive distributions obey
\begin{align}
T_n
&:=\|p_\vartheta(\cdot\mid y_{1:n-1})
      -p_{\theta_0}(\cdot\mid y_{1:n-1})\|_{\mathrm{TV}}\notag\\*
&\leq1-e^{-L\delta}\leq L\delta.
\label{eq:offtruth-predictive-tv}
\end{align}
This argument only uses the density lower comparison on the generating
support; candidate mass outside that support is included in $T_n$.
\endgraf
Now condition under the actual data law. The preceding centering and
bounds imply
\begin{align}
d_n&:=\E_{\theta_0}
 [e_n^\alpha(\vartheta)\mid\mathcal F_{n-1}^Y]\notag\\*
&=\int e_n^\alpha(\vartheta;y)
 \{p_{\theta_0}(y\mid Y_{1:n-1})
   -p_\vartheta(y\mid Y_{1:n-1})\}\,\nu(dy),\notag\\
\|d_n\|_2&\leq2K(1-\alpha)T_n
\leq2KL(1-\alpha)\delta.
\label{eq:offtruth-predictable-drift}
\end{align}
Put $m_n=e_n^\alpha(\vartheta)-d_n$. These are martingale differences
under $P_{\theta_0}$, so
\begin{align}
\E_{\theta_0}\left\|\sum_{n=2}^N m_n\right\|_2^2
&=\sum_{n=2}^N\E_{\theta_0}\|m_n\|_2^2\notag\\*
&=\sum_{n=2}^N\left\{
 \E_{\theta_0}\|e_n^\alpha(\vartheta)\|_2^2
 -\E_{\theta_0}\|d_n\|_2^2\right\}\notag\\*
&\leq(N-1)K^2(1-\alpha)^2.
\end{align}
Since $e_1^\alpha=0$, the $L^2$ triangle inequality gives
\[
\left\|\sum_{n=1}^N e_n^\alpha(\vartheta)\right\|_{L^2(P_{\theta_0})}
\leq K(1-\alpha)\sqrt{N-1}
   +2KL(1-\alpha)(N-1)\delta,
\]
which proves the bound. Squaring uses $(a+c)^2\leq2a^2+2c^2$.}
\end{proof}

\kevin{\normalfont\normalsize
The linear order in $N$ therefore persists for fixed parameter sequences
satisfying $\|\vartheta_N-\theta_0\|_2=O(N^{-1/2})$.
At a fixed nonzero distance, the drift can contribute order $N^2$.
The proposition is pointwise in the evaluation parameter. Applying it
to an optimization iterate chosen using the same observations requires
additional uniform control.}

\begin{remark}[Fixed observations and random observations]
\label{rem:continuous-population-sharpness}
\kevin{\normalfont\normalsize
A continuous-state example shows why the two population bounds have
different powers of $N$. Let $X_n\in[0,1]$, write $s(x)=2x-1$, and take
$|\theta|<1/2$. Initialize $X_0$ uniformly on $[0,1]$, independently
of $\theta$ and all later draws. At odd times reset $X_n$ to an independent uniform draw.
At even times use the transition density
\begin{equation}
q(z\mid x)=1+\tfrac12s(x)s(z).
\label{eq:continuous-sharpness-transition}
\end{equation}
The observations are Bernoulli, with success probabilities
\begin{equation}
\Pr_\theta(Y_n=1\mid X_n=x)
=\begin{cases}
\tfrac12\{1+\theta s(x)\},&n\text{ odd},\\
\tfrac12\{1+\tfrac12s(x)\},&n\text{ even}.
\end{cases}
\label{eq:continuous-sharpness-measurement}
\end{equation}
The parameter enters only the odd-time measurement model.
Each two-observation block is independent of the others. For its
observation signs $a=2Y_{2i-1}-1$ and $b=2Y_{2i}-1$, integration gives
\begin{align}
\Pr_\theta(a,b)
&=\int_0^1\!\int_0^1
\frac{1+a\theta s(x)}2
\left\{1+\tfrac12s(x)s(z)\right\}
\frac{1+\tfrac12 b s(z)}2\,dz\,dx\notag\\*
&=\frac14\left(1+\frac{ab\theta}{36}\right),
\label{eq:continuous-sharpness-pair-likelihood}
\end{align}
using $\int s(x)\,dx=0$ and $\int s(x)^2\,dx=1/3$.
At $\theta=\phi=0$, $h_{2i-1}=a s(X_{2i-1})$ and $h_{2i}=0$.
The odd observation leaves the state uniform, while the even observation
changes the posterior mean of $h_{2i-1}$ from zero to $ab/36$.
All other positive-lag coefficients vanish because the next reset
separates the blocks. Thus Equation~
(\ref{eq:population-conditional-score-path-decomposition}) gives
full and discounted block scores $ab/36$ and $\alpha ab/36$.
For $N=2m$,
\begin{equation}
\nabla_\theta\ell(0)-\nabla_\theta\ell^\alpha(0)
=\frac{1-\alpha}{36}\sum_{i=1}^m a_i b_i.
\label{eq:continuous-sharpness-bias}
\end{equation}
For the fixed sequence $Y_n=1$, the squared difference is exactly
\begin{equation}
\left|\nabla_\theta\ell(0)-\nabla_\theta\ell^\alpha(0)\right|^2
=\frac{N^2(1-\alpha)^2}{5184}.
\label{eq:continuous-fixed-data-sharpness}
\end{equation}
Under the generating parameter $0$, the four outcomes of each block
have probability $1/4$. The products $a_i b_i$ are therefore independent
with mean zero and variance one, giving
\begin{equation}
\E_0\left|\nabla_\theta\ell(0)-\nabla_\theta\ell^\alpha(0)\right|^2
=\frac{N(1-\alpha)^2}{2592}.
\label{eq:continuous-averaged-data-sharpness}
\end{equation}
Both orders are consequently attained within the stated model class.
\endgraf
The same example shows that the additional $N^2\delta^2$ term in
Proposition~\ref{prop:population-offtruth} is necessary. Allow both the
generating parameter $\theta_0$ and the fixed evaluation/reference
parameter $\vartheta$ to lie in $(-1/2,1/2)$. Write $c_i=a_i b_i$.
The block likelihood in
Equation~(\ref{eq:continuous-sharpness-pair-likelihood}) gives
\[
\Pr_{\theta_0}(c_i=c)=\frac{1+c\theta_0/36}{2},\qquad c\in\{-1,1\},
\qquad
Z_i(\vartheta)=\frac{c_i}{36+c_i\vartheta}
\]
for its exact score. The odd-time marginal probability is $1/2$ at every
parameter, so its score and $\rho_{2i-1}^\vartheta(h_{2i-1}^\vartheta)$
are zero. The even-time measurement has no parameter dependence.
There is consequently only one nonzero lag contribution per block, and
the discounted score is $\alpha Z_i(\vartheta)$. Summing the two possible
values gives
\begin{align}
\E_{\theta_0}Z_i(\vartheta)
&=\frac12\left\{\frac{1+\theta_0/36}{36+\vartheta}
 -\frac{1-\theta_0/36}{36-\vartheta}\right\}
=\frac{\theta_0-\vartheta}{1296-\vartheta^2},\notag\\
\Var_{\theta_0}Z_i(\vartheta)
&=\frac12\left\{\frac{1+\theta_0/36}{(36+\vartheta)^2}
 +\frac{1-\theta_0/36}{(36-\vartheta)^2}\right\}
 -\left(\frac{\theta_0-\vartheta}{1296-\vartheta^2}\right)^2
=\frac{1296-\theta_0^2}{(1296-\vartheta^2)^2}.
\end{align}
For $N=2m$, independence of the blocks now gives the exact expression
\begin{align}
&\E_{\theta_0}\left|
\nabla_\theta\ell(\vartheta)-\nabla_\theta\ell^\alpha(\vartheta)
\right|^2\notag\\*
&\quad=(1-\alpha)^2
\frac{m(1296-\theta_0^2)+m^2(\theta_0-\vartheta)^2}
     {(1296-\vartheta^2)^2}.
\label{eq:continuous-offtruth-sharpness}
\end{align}
Thus both $N$ and $N^2\|\vartheta-\theta_0\|^2$ are attained, even
with independent observation blocks. For $\alpha>0$, this example's
discounted score has the same zeros and direction as the exact score;
the quadratic term describes score error, not a failure of optimization.
\endgraf
To check the assumptions, transition densities lie in $[1/2,3/2]$,
measurement probabilities in $[1/4,3/4]$, and the first two log-measurement
parameter derivatives are bounded by $2$ and $4$. The simulator can
use the inverse conditional CDF
\[
F(z\mid x)=z+\tfrac12s(x)(z^2-z),\qquad
\left|\frac{\partial F^{-1}(u\mid x)}{\partial x}\right|
=\frac{z(1-z)}{q(z\mid x)}\leq\frac12.
\]
The odd-time Jacobian is zero and the spatial log-measurement derivative
is at most $2$, so (A6) holds with $C=2$ and $\kappa=1/2$.
The positive CDF derivative gives a smooth extension at the interval
endpoints. Since the simulator is independent of $\theta$, its parameter
tangent is zero. The joint process $(X_n,0)$ has the same transition
bounds relative to $dx\otimes\delta_0$, proving (A6-J).
Smoothness gives (A1) and (A5). For each fixed $N$, the deterministic
bounds $G^*=2N^2$ and $H^*=8\sum_{n=1}^N n^2$ suffice for (A4), uniformly
in $J$ and $\alpha$. The model and stability constants used in the rate
bounds are independent of $N$.}
\end{remark}

\subsection{Particle error}

\kevin{\normalfont\normalsize
\paragraph*{(A6) Measurement-sensitivity decay.}
\label{assump:measurement-sensitivity}
The simulator is $C^1$ in its incoming state. For a continuation
$X_n^\theta(x_t,\omega_{t+1:n})$ from $x_t$, with $X_t^\theta(x_t)=x_t$,
\begin{equation}
\left\|
D_{x_t}\log f_{Y_n\mid X_n}
\bigl(y_n^*\mid X_n^\theta(x_t,\omega_{t+1:n});\theta\bigr)
\right\|_2
\leq C\kappa^{n-t},\qquad C<\infty,\quad 0<\kappa<1.
\label{eq:measurement-sensitivity-assumption}
\end{equation}
The bound is uniform in $t\leq n$, states, draws, and $\theta$ near $\phi$.
The parameter derivatives of the initial state, simulator, and log measurement
density are bounded, holding incoming states fixed.
\endgraf
\paragraph*{(A6-J) Joint-transition alternative.}
\label{assump:joint-gradient-density}
The log measurement density is $C^1$ in $(x,\theta)$.
The joint process $(X_n^\theta,D_n^\theta)$,
$D_n^\theta=\partial_\theta X_n^\theta$, has
transition densities satisfying
\begin{equation}
0<\underbar M_D
\leq f_{(X_n,D_n)\mid(X_{n-1},D_{n-1})}
(x',v'\mid x,v;\theta)
\leq\bar M_D<\infty
\label{eq:joint-gradient-density-assumption}
\end{equation}
relative to a common finite measure on the invariant joint state space,
uniformly in time, admissible pairs, and $\theta$ near $\phi$.
\endgraf
The particle bounds assume either (A6) or (A6-J); the consistency results
and population Lemmas~\ref{lem:derivative-measure-forgetting}
and~\ref{lem:dmop-population-discount-bias} retain (A1)--(A5).
Neither additional condition requires density evaluation by the algorithm.
Their constants enter the particle bounds, while $\varrho$ retains its
physical-model definition.
\endgraf
\paragraph*{Sufficient condition for (A6).}
Suppose the parameter derivatives in (A6) are bounded, the spatial log
measurement derivatives are bounded by $L_g$, and the one-step simulator
Jacobians are bounded by $L$. If, for fixed $b\geq1$ and $q\in(0,1)$,
$\|\partial X_{t+b}^\theta/\partial X_t^\theta\|_{\rm op}\leq q$
uniformly with the draws fixed, then writing $n-t=mb+v$, $0\leq v<b$,
gives
\begin{equation}
\left\|D_{x_t}\log f_{Y_n\mid X_n}(y_n^*\mid X_n^\theta;\theta)\right\|_2
\leq L_g L^v q^m
\leq C\kappa^{n-t},\qquad \kappa=q^{1/b}\in(0,1).
\label{eq:measurement-sensitivity-block-criterion}
\end{equation}
Here $C=L_g\max_{0\leq v<b}(L/\kappa)^v$; a zero block Jacobian permits
any $q\in(0,1)$.
\endgraf
\paragraph*{Example: physical mixing without (A6).}
Consider the annulus $\mathcal X=\{x\in\mathbb R^2:1\leq\|x\|_2\leq2\}$.
Let $R_u$ denote planar rotation by $u$, and let independent simulator
draws satisfy $U_n\sim\operatorname{Unif}[0,2\pi]$ and
$V_n\sim\operatorname{Unif}[1,2]$. Set
\begin{equation}
X_0^\theta=R_\theta x_0,\qquad
X_n^\theta=V_n R_{U_n}\frac{X_{n-1}^\theta}{\|X_{n-1}^\theta\|_2},
\qquad
\Pr(Y_n=1\mid X_n=x)=\frac12+\frac{x_1}{4\|x\|_2}.
\label{eq:gradient-stability-rotation-example}
\end{equation}
The physical transition forgets the incoming state in one step: its
Lebesgue density on $\mathcal X$ is
\begin{equation}
f_{X_n\mid X_{n-1}}(x'\mid x;\theta)=\frac{1}{2\pi\|x'\|_2},
\qquad \frac1{4\pi}\leq f_{X_n\mid X_{n-1}}\leq\frac1{2\pi}.
\end{equation}
Both measurement probabilities lie in $[1/4,3/4]$.
For fixed draws, $X_n^\theta=R_\theta X_n^0$, so all parameter
derivatives exist and are bounded; in particular the first two log
measurement derivatives have magnitudes at most $1$ and $2$.
The likelihood is smooth, and the MOP derivatives are bounded for each
fixed horizon. Thus (A1)--(A5) hold.
\endgraf
Nevertheless, fix the observations $y_n^*=1$. For $n>t$, the composition is
\begin{equation}
X_n^\theta(x,\omega_{t+1:n})
=V_nR_{U_{t+1}+\cdots+U_n}\frac{x}{\|x\|_2}.
\end{equation}
Its final angle is uniform, and a fixed-random-number change in the
incoming angle is preserved. Differentiating the displayed measurement
probability therefore gives
\begin{equation}
\E\left\|D_x\log f_{Y_n\mid X_n}
\bigl(1\mid X_n^\theta(x,\omega_{t+1:n});\theta\bigr)\right\|_2^2
=\frac{1}{2\pi\|x\|_2^2}
\int_0^{2\pi}\frac{\sin^2 u}{(2+\cos u)^2}\,du>0.
\label{eq:gradient-stability-rotation-nondecay}
\end{equation}
The right side is independent of $n-t$, so (A6) fails despite (A1)--(A5).
Here $D_n^\theta=R_{\pi/2}X_n^\theta$, and the joint transition has the
physical density relative to $dx'\,\delta_{R_{\pi/2}x'}(dv')$.
Thus (A6-J) holds and the particle bounds still apply. In particular,
(A6-J) does not imply (A6).
\endgraf
Under (A6-J), the measurement gradient is
\begin{equation}
h_{t,i}(x,v)=\partial_{\theta_i}\log f_{Y_t\mid X_t}(y_t^*\mid x;\theta)
+\sum_{a=1}^d\partial_{x_a}\log f_{Y_t\mid X_t}(y_t^*\mid x;\theta)v_{ai},
\qquad \theta=\phi,
\label{eq:joint-current-measurement-gradient}
\end{equation}
which is bounded by (A3).
}

\begin{lem}[Particle error of the DMOP-$\alpha$ score]
\label{lem:dmop-alpha-variance}
\label{lem:dmop-full-particle-bias}
Suppose $J\geq2$, $\theta=\phi$, $\alpha\in[0,1]$, Assumptions
{\refAssumpBoundedProcess}, {\refAssumpBoundedMeasurement}, and
{\refAssumpDifferentiableSimulator} hold, and multinomial resampling is
used. Suppose also that either (A6) or (A6-J) holds. Then
\kevin{\normalfont\normalsize for either the before- or after-resampling estimator,
\begin{align}
\E\|\nabla_\theta\hat\ell^\alpha(\theta)-\nabla_\theta\ell^\alpha(\theta)\|_2^2
&\lesssim pG'(\theta)^2
\left[\frac{N}{J}\min\left\{\sum_{r=0}^{N-1}\alpha^r,J\right\}
+\frac{N^2}{J^2}\right],
\label{eq:review-alpha-particle-mse}\\
\|\E\nabla_\theta\hat\ell^\alpha(\theta)-\nabla_\theta\ell^\alpha(\theta)\|_2
&\lesssim N\sqrt p\,G'(\theta)/J.
\label{eq:review-alpha-particle-bias}
\end{align}
Both estimators have the same expectation. Under (A6), use the enlarged
$G'(\theta)$ in Equation~(\ref{eq:review-component-normalization}).
The MSE bound also bounds the variance.}
The constants suppressed by $\lesssim$ depend only on the fixed model
and stability bounds and are independent of $N,J$, and $\alpha$.
\end{lem}

\begin{proof}[Proof.]
\kevin{\normalfont\normalsize We first decompose the measurement gradient under (A6), then separate
the particle error into its conditional mean and genealogical parts.}

\kevin{\normalfont\normalsize \medskip\noindent\emph{Gradient-age decomposition.}\quad
All derivatives are evaluated at $\theta=\phi$, with reference
resampling indices fixed. Write
$X_u^\theta=\process_u(X_{u-1}^\theta;\theta,\omega_u)$, and let
$\partial_{\theta_i}\process_u$ denote its partial derivative holding
$X_{u-1}^\theta$ fixed. The chain rule, iterated from time zero, gives
\begin{equation}
\partial_{\theta_i}X_s^\theta
=
\frac{\partial X_s^\theta}{\partial X_0^\theta}
\partial_{\theta_i}X_0^\theta
+\sum_{u=1}^s
\frac{\partial X_s^\theta}{\partial X_u^\theta}
\partial_{\theta_i}\process_u(X_{u-1}^\theta;\theta,\omega_u).
\label{eq:simulator-gradient-chain-expansion}
\end{equation}
Under (A6), write the coordinate-$i$ measurement gradient as a sum
over the age $r$ of its parameter derivative:
\begin{align}
h_{s,i}^{[0]}
&=
\left.\partial_{\theta_i}\log f_{Y_s\mid X_s}(y_s^*\mid x;\theta)
\right|_{x=X_s^\theta}
+\left.D_x\log f_{Y_s\mid X_s}(y_s^*\mid x;\theta)\right|_{x=X_s^\theta}
\partial_{\theta_i}\process_s(X_{s-1}^\theta;\theta,\omega_s),
\notag\\
h_{s,i}^{[r]}
&=
\left.
D_x\log f_{Y_s\mid X_s}
\bigl(y_s^*\mid X_s^\theta(x,\omega_{s-r+1:s});\theta\bigr)
\right|_{x=X_{s-r}^\theta}
\partial_{\theta_i}\process_{s-r}(X_{s-r-1}^\theta;\theta,\omega_{s-r}),
\quad 1\leq r<s,
\notag\\
h_{s,i}^{[s]}
&=
\left.
D_x\log f_{Y_s\mid X_s}
\bigl(y_s^*\mid X_s^\theta(x,\omega_{1:s});\theta\bigr)
\right|_{x=X_0^\theta}
\partial_{\theta_i}X_0^\theta.
\label{eq:measurement-gradient-age-components}
\end{align}
The first term includes the direct measurement derivative and the
current simulator derivative; the last includes the initial-state derivative.
Under (A6), enlarge $G'(\theta)$ if necessary so that
\begin{equation}
\max\left\{
 \|\partial_{\theta_i}X_0^\theta\|_2,\,
 \|\partial_{\theta_i}\process_u(x;\theta,\omega_u)\|_2,\,
 \left|\partial_{\theta_i}\log f_{Y_u\mid X_u}(y_u^*\mid x;\theta)\right|
\right\}\leq G'(\theta),
\label{eq:review-component-normalization}
\end{equation}
uniformly in $u,i,x$ and simulator draws, holding $x$ fixed in the
partial derivatives. Under (A6-J), the original bound on $h_{s,i}$ suffices.
Multiplying Equation~(\ref{eq:simulator-gradient-chain-expansion}) by
the spatial log measurement derivative gives
\begin{equation}
h_{s,i}=\sum_{r=0}^s h_{s,i}^{[r]},
\qquad
\|h_{s,i}^{[r]}\|_\infty\leq CG'(\theta)\kappa^r.
\label{eq:measurement-gradient-age-sum}
\end{equation}
For $r=0$ use (A6) with $t=s$ and the bounded partial derivatives;
for $1\leq r<s$ use it with $t=s-r$; for $r=s$ use $t=0$ and the
initial-gradient bound. For $r<s$, the displayed function depends only
on $X_{s-r-1}$ and the simulator variables from $s-r$ through $s$.
For $r=s$ it also uses the initial simulator draw, whose particle
replicates are independent. Set $h_{s,i}^{[r]}=0$ for $r>s$.
\endgraf
Under (A6-J), take instead $h_{s,i}^{[0]}=h_{s,i}$, the bounded current
function in Equation~(\ref{eq:joint-current-measurement-gradient}), and
$h_{s,i}^{[r]}=0$ for $r\geq1$. Use the joint pair as the state throughout
the particle calculation. The sums over $r$ below then contain only
$r=0$; $\kappa$ is needed only under (A6).}

\kevin{\normalfont\normalsize \medskip\noindent\emph{After-resampling score.}\quad
We first treat the after-resampling score $\nabla_\theta\hat\ell_J^{A,\alpha}$, then transfer the bound to
$\nabla_\theta\hat\ell_J^{B,\alpha}$. Put
$H_N(\alpha)=\sum_{q=0}^{N-1}\alpha^q$.
Lemma~\ref{lem:dmop-full-score-summation-by-parts} gives
\begin{align}
\nabla_\theta\hat\ell_J^{A,\alpha}
&=\sum_{s=1}^N\sum_{b=s}^N c_b\alpha^{b-s}m(\xi_b)(h_s),
&c_b&=\begin{cases}1-\alpha,&b<N,\\1,&b=N.\end{cases}
\label{eq:logfree-endpoints}
\end{align}
Replacing $m(\xi_b)$ by $\rho_b$ gives $\nabla_\theta\ell^\alpha$.
For each creation time $s$, the endpoint weights satisfy
\begin{align}
\sum_{b=s}^N c_b\alpha^{b-s}&=1,\notag\\
\sum_{b=s}^N c_b\alpha^{b-s}(b-s+1)
&=\sum_{q=0}^{N-s}\sum_{b=s+q}^N c_b\alpha^{b-s}
=\sum_{q=0}^{N-s}\alpha^q
\leq H_N(\alpha).
\label{eq:logfree-endpoint-moments}
\end{align}
These identities include $\alpha=0,1$. Fix coordinate $i$ and age $r$.
Under (A6), Equation~(\ref{eq:measurement-gradient-age-sum}) gives
$\|h_{s,i}^{[r]}\|_\infty\leq CG'(\theta)\kappa^r$ and a block starting at
$a_s=\max(1,s-r-1)$, with the initial history included when needed.
Under (A6-J), use the joint state, $a_s=s$, and only $r=0$.
Write $Z_r$ for this component of \eqref{eq:logfree-endpoints} and $z_r$
for its population value.
\endgraf
We separate the error in the prediction clouds from the remaining
random genealogy. Under (A6), let
\[
\mathcal C=\sigma(X_{u,j}^{P,\phi}:1\leq u\leq N+1,\ 1\leq j\leq J).
\]
Under (A6-J), use the joint states instead. The artificial transition
at $N+1$ is to a one-point space with no observation, and its parents
record the actual time-$N$ resampling. Parent indices and simulator
draws are excluded from $\mathcal C$. We will bound the two terms in
\[
\E|Z_r-z_r|^2
=\E|\E[Z_r\mid\mathcal C]-z_r|^2+\E\Var(Z_r\mid\mathcal C).
\]
For $2\leq u\leq N$, the conditional parent probabilities are
\begin{equation}
B_u^J(j,l)=
\frac{g_{u-1,l}^{\phi}
 f_{X_u\mid X_{u-1}}(X_{u,j}^{P,\phi}\mid X_{u-1,l}^{P,\phi};\phi)}
{\sum_vg_{u-1,v}^{\phi}
 f_{X_u\mid X_{u-1}}(X_{u,j}^{P,\phi}\mid X_{u-1,v}^{P,\phi};\phi)}.
\label{eq:logfree-backward}
\end{equation}
Under (A6-J), use the joint transition density. At the artificial last
layer, the transition density is one, and hence
\[
B_{N+1}^J(j,l)=g_{N,l}^{\phi}/\sum_v g_{N,v}^{\phi}.
\]
Let $K_{u-1,j}$ be the parent of node $(u,j)$ and $R_u(x,x';d\omega)$
the simulator law conditional on its two states. Bayes' rule gives
\begin{align*}
&\Pr\{K_{u-1,j}=l_{u,j},\ \omega_{u,j}\in d\omega_{u,j}
                 \text{ for all }u\geq2,j\mid\mathcal C\}\\
&\qquad=\prod_{u=2}^{N+1}\prod_{j=1}^J
 B_u^J(j,l_{u,j})
 R_u(X_{u-1,l_{u,j}}^{P,\phi},X_{u,j}^{P,\phi};d\omega_{u,j}).
\end{align*}
The initial histories likewise factor over first-layer nodes, and the
last-layer draw is deterministic. Thus distinct nodes have independent
parents and draws conditional on $\mathcal C$; a shared node has one
shared parent and draw. Under (A6-J), omit the draws.
The density bounds give
\begin{equation}
\max_{j,l}B_u^J(j,l)\leq C/J,\qquad
\operatorname{osc}(B_u^Jf)\leq\varrho_*\operatorname{osc}(f),\qquad 0\leq\varrho_*<1.
\label{eq:logfree-backward-bounds}
\end{equation}
Here $\varrho_*<1$ bounds both backward and exact forward conditional
contraction. It need not equal the population-bias coefficient $\varrho$.
\endgraf
For a fixed state block, integrate the simulator bridges:
\[
f_{s,r}(x_{a_s:s})
=\int h_{s,i}^{[r]}(x_{a_s:s},\omega)
       \prod_{v=a_s+1}^{s}R_v(x_{v-1},x_v;d\omega_v).
\]
When $a_s=1$, this integral also includes the initial history conditional
on $x_1$. Under (A6-J), $f_{s,0}=h_{s,i}$ on the current joint state.
The bridge kernels are fixed model kernels, and
$\|f_{s,r}\|_\infty\leq CG'(\theta)\kappa^r$. Thus
\[
\E[m(\xi_b)(h_{s,i}^{[r]})\mid\mathcal C]
=\frac1J\sum_{j=1}^J\int f_{s,r}(x_{a_s:s})
       \prod_{v=2}^{b+1}B_v^J(x_v,dx_{v-1}),
\qquad x_{b+1}=X_{b+1,j}^{P,\phi}.
\]
The corresponding exact integral is $\rho_b(h_{s,i}^{[r]})$, since
no observation is added at $b+1$.
\endgraf
\emph{Error of the conditional mean.}
For this calculation return to the ordinary particle law. Let
$\mathcal H_u$ be the filtration of prediction clouds through $u$,
$\mathcal H_0$ trivial, and $\mu_u$ the prediction law given
$\mathcal H_{u-1}$. The empirical measure $\eta_u^J$ averages $J$
conditionally iid draws from $\mu_u$.
We use the telescope of Definition 5.1 and equation (7.1) of
\citet{delmoral10backward}, written explicitly as follows.
Fix $(s,b,r)$, put $T=b+1$, and write $M_{v+1}$ for the exact
transition and $g_v$ for the measurement density.
Extend \eqref{eq:logfree-backward} to a kernel $B_v^J(x_v,dx_{v-1})$
using the cloud at $v-1$. Define
\begin{align*}
D_{u,T}^Jf(x_u)
&=\int f(x_{1:T})
 \prod_{v=2}^{u}B_v^J(x_v,dx_{v-1})
 \prod_{v=u}^{T-1}g_v(x_v)M_{v+1}(x_v,dx_{v+1}),\\
Q_{u,T}1(x_u)&=D_{u,T}^J1(x_u),\qquad
A_u=\frac{\eta_u^J(D_{u,T}^Jf_{s,r})}{\eta_u^J(Q_{u,T}1)}.
\end{align*}
For $u\geq2$, Bayes' formula gives
\[
\mu_u(dx_u)B_u^J(x_u,dx_{u-1})
=\frac{\eta_{u-1}^J(dx_{u-1})g_{u-1}(x_{u-1})
                 M_u(x_{u-1},dx_u)}{\eta_{u-1}^J(g_{u-1})}.
\]
Hence
\begin{align*}
\mu_u(D_{u,T}^Jf_{s,r})
&=\frac{\eta_{u-1}^J(D_{u-1,T}^Jf_{s,r})}
