             {\eta_{u-1}^J(g_{u-1})},\\*
\mu_u(Q_{u,T}1)
&=\frac{\eta_{u-1}^J(Q_{u-1,T}1)}{\eta_{u-1}^J(g_{u-1})}.
\end{align*}
Their ratio is $A_{u-1}$. At $u=1$, the exact initial prediction law
gives $A_0=\rho_b(h_{s,i}^{[r]})$; at $u=T$, $Q_{T,T}1=1$ and
$A_T$ is the conditional smoother. Thus, with $e_{u;s,b}=A_u-A_{u-1}$,
\begin{equation}
\E[m(\xi_b)(h_{s,i}^{[r]})\mid\mathcal C]
 -\rho_b(h_{s,i}^{[r]})=\sum_{u=1}^{b+1}e_{u;s,b}.
\label{eq:logfree-dds}
\end{equation}
For its ratio expansion, put
\begin{align}
q_{u;b}&=\frac{Q_{u,b+1}1}{\mu_u(Q_{u,b+1}1)},
&F_{u;s,b}&=\frac{D_{u,b+1}^Jf_{s,r}}{Q_{u,b+1}1},\notag\\
d_{u;s,b}&=q_{u;b}\{F_{u;s,b}-\mu_u(q_{u;b}F_{u;s,b})\}.
\end{align}
These functions use empirical past kernels and exact future kernels,
so are $\mathcal H_{u-1}$-measurable. The ratio expansion gives
\begin{align}
\mu_uq_{u;b}&=1,&\mu_ud_{u;s,b}&=0,\notag\\
e_{u;s,b}
&=(\eta_u^J-\mu_u)d_{u;s,b}
-\frac{\{(\eta_u^J-\mu_u)d_{u;s,b}\}
        \{(\eta_u^J-\mu_u)q_{u;b}\}}{\eta_u^Jq_{u;b}}.
\label{eq:logfree-ratio}
\end{align}
For $v<T$, the exact forward kernel conditional on observations
through $b=T-1$ is
\begin{equation}
P_v^{(b)}(x,dz)=
\frac{M_{v+1}(x,dz)Q_{v+1,T}1(z)}
     {M_{v+1}(Q_{v+1,T}1)(x)}.
\label{eq:weak-bias-forward-normalized-kernel}
\end{equation}
If $\lambda$ is the transition reference measure and
$\epsilon=\underbar M/\bar M$, then
\begin{align}
P_v^{(b)}(x,dz)
&\geq\epsilon\frac{Q_{v+1,T}1(z)\lambda(dz)}
 {\int Q_{v+1,T}1(w)\lambda(dw)},
\label{eq:weak-bias-forward-minorization}\\
\operatorname{osc}(P_v^{(b)}f)&\leq(1-\epsilon)\operatorname{osc}(f).
\label{eq:weak-bias-normalized-semigroup-contraction}
\end{align}
Use the joint transition bounds under (A6-J). At the artificial final
transition the kernel is constant, so the same contraction bound holds.
The current potential cancels in this conditional kernel. Thus
\begin{align*}
F_{u;s,b}&=P_u^{(b)}\cdots P_{a_s-1}^{(b)}F_{a_s;s,b},&&u<a_s,\\
F_{u;s,b}&=B_u^J\cdots B_{s+1}^JF_{s;s,b},&&u>s.
\end{align*}
Inside the block, $F_{u;s,b}$ is a conditional average of $f_{s,r}$.
These identities and the forward and backward contractions give
\[
\operatorname{osc}(F_{u;s,b})\leq 2CG'(\theta)\kappa^r
\begin{cases}
\varrho_*^{a_s-u},&u<a_s,\\
1,&a_s\leq u\leq s,\\
\varrho_*^{u-s},&u>s.
\end{cases}
\]
The bounds on transition and measurement densities give
\begin{equation}
\frac{\sup_x Q_{u,T}1(x)}{\inf_x Q_{u,T}1(x)}
\leq\frac{\bar G\bar M}{\underbar G\underbar M},\qquad u<T.
\label{eq:weak-bias-uniform-normalizer-ratio}
\end{equation}
with the joint transition bounds under (A6-J). Since $Q_{T,T}1=1$,
this controls $q_{u;b}$ for every $u$. Also,
\[
|F_{u;s,b}-\mu_u(q_{u;b}F_{u;s,b})|\leq\operatorname{osc}(F_{u;s,b}).
\]
It follows that
\begin{equation}
C^{-1}\leq q_{u;b}\leq C,\qquad
\operatorname{osc}(d_{u;s,b})\leq CG'(\theta)\kappa^r
 \varrho_*^{\operatorname{dist}(u,[a_s,s])}.
\label{eq:logfree-local-bounds}
\end{equation}
At $N+1$, $\eta_{N+1}^J=\mu_{N+1}$ and the local error is zero.
\endgraf
Sum the linear terms before taking second moments:
\[
d_u^{[r]}=\sum_{s=1}^N\sum_{b=\max(s,u-1)}^N
 c_b\alpha^{b-s}d_{u;s,b}.
\]
Each inner sum has weight at most one, and at most $r+2$ blocks
contain any fixed $u$. Therefore
\begin{align}
\sum_{s=1}^N\varrho_*^{\operatorname{dist}(u,[a_s,s])}
&\leq r+2+\frac{2}{1-\varrho_*}\leq C(r+2),\notag\\
\operatorname{osc}(d_u^{[r]})&\leq CG'(\theta)\kappa^r(r+2).
\end{align}
Set $L_u=(\eta_u^J-\mu_u)d_u^{[r]}$. Conditional iid sampling yields
\begin{align}
\E[L_u\mid\mathcal H_{u-1}]&=0,\notag\\
\E[L_u^2\mid\mathcal H_{u-1}]
&=\frac{\mu_u\{(d_u^{[r]}-\mu_ud_u^{[r]})^2\}}{J}
\leq\frac{CG'(\theta)^2\kappa^{2r}(r+2)^2}{J},\notag\\
\E\left|\sum_uL_u\right|^2
&=\sum_u\E L_u^2
\leq\frac{CG'(\theta)^2\kappa^{2r}(r+2)^2N}{J}.
\label{eq:logfree-linear}
\end{align}
The cross terms vanish because, for $v<u$, $L_v$ is
$\mathcal H_{u-1}$-measurable and
$\E[L_vL_u]=\E[L_v\E(L_u\mid\mathcal H_{u-1})]=0$.
\endgraf
For the quadratic term, conditional iid sampling of a bounded function
$f$ gives
\[
\E[\{(\eta_u^J-\mu_u)f\}^4\mid\mathcal H_{u-1}]
=\frac{\mu_u(\bar f^4)}{J^3}
 +\frac{3(J-1)\mu_u(\bar f^2)^2}{J^3}
\leq\frac{4\operatorname{osc}(f)^4}{J^2},\quad \bar f=f-\mu_uf.
\]
Conditional Cauchy--Schwarz and the denominator bound therefore imply
\begin{equation}
\left\|\frac{\{(\eta_u^J-\mu_u)d_{u;s,b}\}
                 \{(\eta_u^J-\mu_u)q_{u;b}\}}
                {\eta_u^Jq_{u;b}}\right\|_{L^2}
\leq\frac{CG'(\theta)\kappa^r}{J}
 \varrho_*^{\operatorname{dist}(u,[a_s,s])}.
\end{equation}
Sum over $u$ and the endpoint weights in \eqref{eq:logfree-endpoint-moments}.
The total remainder has $L^2$ norm at most
$CG'(\theta)\kappa^r(r+2)N/J$. Since the linear terms are centered,
\eqref{eq:logfree-linear} gives
\begin{align}
\E|\E[Z_r\mid\mathcal C]-z_r|^2
&\leq CG'(\theta)^2\kappa^{2r}(r+2)^2
 \left(\frac NJ+\frac{N^2}{J^2}\right),\notag\\
|\E Z_r-z_r|&\leq CG'(\theta)\kappa^r(r+2)N/J.
\label{eq:logfree-mean}
\end{align}
\endgraf
\emph{Conditional genealogical variance.}
Condition on $\mathcal C$ and reveal parents and simulator draws
backwards, one layer at a time.
For endpoint $b+1$, let $W_{b,u}(j)$ be the fraction of its terminal
labels at node $j$ in layer $u$, defined only for $u\leq b+1$.
Initially $W_{b,b+1}(j)=1/J$. Let
$\mathcal R_u=\sigma(\mathcal C,V_{v,j}:v>u,\ 1\leq j\leq J)$,
where $V_{v,j}$ records that node's parent and simulator draw. Then
\[
W_{b,u-1}(l)=\sum_jW_{b,u}(j)\mathbf1_{\{K_{u-1,j}=l\}},\qquad u\geq2.
\]
The weights on the right are $\mathcal R_u$-measurable, and the parent
choices are conditionally independent. Hence
\begin{align}
\E\left[\sum_jW_{b,u-1}(j)^2\mid\mathcal R_u\right]
&=\sum_jW_{b,u}(j)^2
 +\sum_{j\ne l}W_{b,u}(j)W_{b,u}(l)
       \sum_vB_u^J(j,v)B_u^J(l,v)\notag\\
&\leq\sum_jW_{b,u}(j)^2+C/J,\notag\\
\E\left[\sum_jW_{b,u}(j)^2\mid\mathcal C\right]
&\leq\min\{1,C(b-u+2)/J\}.
\label{eq:logfree-descendants}
\end{align}
The weights sum to one, giving the cap. Define
\[
c_{s,r}(u)=\mathbf1_{\{a_s<u\leq s+1\}}
 +\varrho_*^{u-s-1}\mathbf1_{\{u>s+1\}},\quad u\geq2,
\qquad c_{s,r}(1)=\mathbf1_{\{a_s=1\}}.
\]
Changing a node within the block costs $CG'(\theta)\kappa^r$.
After the block, backward contraction supplies the geometric factor;
earlier choices have no effect once the block is revealed. Thus
\[
\sup_u\sum_{s=1}^N c_{s,r}(u)\leq C(r+2).
\]
Let $V_{u,j}$ be the newly revealed parent and simulator draw at node
$j$, or its initial-history draw when $u=1$. Let
$\mathcal D_{b,u}(j)$ be the terminal labels at $b+1$ whose revealed
paths pass through node $j$ at time $u$, so
$|\mathcal D_{b,u}(j)|=JW_{b,u}(j)$. Their conditional contribution is
\begin{align}
Z_{u,j}(v)
&=\frac1J\sum_s\sum_{b=\max(s,u-1)}^N c_b\alpha^{b-s}
 \sum_{\ell\in\mathcal D_{b,u}(j)}
 \E[h_{s,i}^{[r]}(\xi_{b,\ell})\mid\mathcal R_u,V_{u,j}=v].
\end{align}
The reverse Doob difference is
\[
D_{u,r}=\E[Z_r\mid\mathcal R_u\vee\sigma(V_{u,j}:1\leq j\leq J)]
       -\E[Z_r\mid\mathcal R_u].
\]
Endpoints with $b<u-1$ use only unrevealed earlier nodes and contribute
zero to this difference. For the remaining endpoints, linearity gives
\[
D_{u,r}=\sum_j\{Z_{u,j}(V_{u,j})-
                    \E[Z_{u,j}(V_{u,j})\mid\mathcal R_u]\}.
\]
The conditional expectations retain each terminal label's revealed
suffix and integrate its earlier history. Every path uses one node at
layer $u$, and distinct nodes are conditionally independent. Hence
$\E[D_{u,r}^2\mid\mathcal R_u]$ is a sum of node variances, with
\[
\operatorname{osc}(Z_{u,j})\leq CG'(\theta)\kappa^r
 \sum_s c_{s,r}(u)\sum_{b=\max(s,u-1)}^N
        c_b\alpha^{b-s}W_{b,u}(j).
\]
Conditional independence first gives
\begin{align*}
\E[D_{u,r}^2\mid\mathcal R_u]
&\leq\sum_j\operatorname{osc}(Z_{u,j})^2,\\
\left\{\E\sum_jW_{b,u}(j)W_{b',u}(j)\mid\mathcal C\right\}^2
&\leq\E\left[\sum_jW_{b,u}(j)^2\mid\mathcal C\right]
       \E\left[\sum_jW_{b',u}(j)^2\mid\mathcal C\right].
\end{align*}
The second line is Cauchy--Schwarz over nodes and probability.
Expanding the square of the oscillation bound and using
\eqref{eq:logfree-descendants} gives
\begin{align}
\E[D_{u,r}^2\mid\mathcal C]
\leq CG'(\theta)^2\kappa^{2r}
\left\{\sum_s c_{s,r}(u)
 \sum_{b=\max(s,u-1)}^N c_b\alpha^{b-s}
       \sqrt{\min\{1,C(b-u+2)/J\}}\right\}^2.
\label{eq:logfree-node}
\end{align}
On a nonzero term, $b-u+2\leq b-s+1+r+2$.
The inner endpoint weights form a subprobability measure. Adding the
missing mass at zero, concavity and \eqref{eq:logfree-endpoint-moments} give
\begin{align}
&\sum_{b=\max(s,u-1)}^N c_b\alpha^{b-s}
       \sqrt{\min\{1,C(b-u+2)/J\}}\notag\\
&\qquad\leq\sqrt{\min\{1,C(H_N(\alpha)+r+2)/J\}}
\leq C\sqrt{(r+3)\min\{1,H_N(\alpha)/J\}}.
\end{align}
Substitute in \eqref{eq:logfree-node}, sum the at most $N+1\leq2N$ layers,
and use reverse martingale orthogonality:
\begin{equation}
\E\Var(Z_r\mid\mathcal C)
\leq CG'(\theta)^2\kappa^{2r}(r+3)^3
       N\min\{1,H_N(\alpha)/J\}.
\label{eq:logfree-genealogy}
\end{equation}
Combine \eqref{eq:logfree-mean} and \eqref{eq:logfree-genealogy} in the
conditional mean--variance identity. Since $H_N\geq1$ and
$\sum_r\kappa^r(r+3)^{3/2}<\infty$, Minkowski over ages and summation
over coordinates prove the after-score MSE and weak-bias bounds.
Under (A6-J), only $r=0$ occurs.
\endgraf
\emph{Before-resampling score.}
At $\theta=\phi$, write the unselected weight gradient as
\[
v_{n,j}=\nabla_\theta\log(g_{n,j}^\theta w_{n,j}^{P,\theta})
=h_{n,j}+\alpha\nabla_\theta\log w_{n-1,j}^{F,\theta},\qquad
W_{n,j}=\frac{g_{n,j}^\phi}{\sum_lg_{n,l}^\phi}.
\]
With multinomial indices $K_{n,j}$, differentiation of the numerator
and denominator of each likelihood increment gives
\begin{align}
\nabla_\theta\log L_n^{B,\theta,\alpha}
&=\sum_jW_{n,j}v_{n,j}
 -\frac{\alpha}{J}\sum_j\nabla_\theta\log w_{n-1,j}^{F,\theta},\notag\\
\nabla_\theta\log L_n^{A,\theta,\alpha}
&=\frac1J\sum_jv_{n,K_{n,j}}
 -\frac{\alpha}{J}\sum_j\nabla_\theta\log w_{n-1,j}^{F,\theta}.
\end{align}
Thus, with $\varepsilon_n=J^{-1}\sum_jv_{n,K_{n,j}}
-\sum_jW_{n,j}v_{n,j}$,
\begin{align}
\nabla_\theta\hat\ell_J^{A,\alpha}-\nabla_\theta\hat\ell_J^{B,\alpha}
&=\sum_{n=1}^N\varepsilon_n,\notag\\
\E[\varepsilon_n\mid\mathcal F_n^-]&=0,\notag\\
\E\left\|\sum_n\varepsilon_n\right\|_2^2
&=\frac1J\sum_n\E\sum_jW_{n,j}
 \left\|v_{n,j}-\sum_lW_{n,l}v_{n,l}\right\|_2^2.
\label{eq:logfree-transfer-identity}
\end{align}
Here $\mathcal F_n^-$ is the complete history before resampling at $n$.
The centered errors are orthogonal in the natural forward filtration,
and the two scores have the same expectation.
\endgraf
To bound the weighted spread in \eqref{eq:logfree-transfer-identity}, condition
on the prediction clouds through $n$ and draw two terminal labels
independently from $W_n$. Trace their actual ancestors backwards. From
\eqref{eq:logfree-backward}, there is $\epsilon>0$ such that
$B_u^J(j,l)\geq\epsilon W_{u-1,l}$. Distinct nodes meet at the next
backward step with probability at least
\[
\sum_l B_u^J(j,l)B_u^J(j',l)
\geq\epsilon^2\sum_lW_{u-1,l}^2\geq\epsilon^2/J.
\]
Once they meet, their earlier histories coincide. With
$\lambda=1-\epsilon^2/J$, their labels $I_t,I'_t$ satisfy
\[
\Pr(I_t\ne I'_t\mid\mathcal C_n)\leq\lambda^{n-t}.
\]
Under (A6-J), put $\Delta_t=h_{t,i}(I_t)-h_{t,i}(I'_t)$.
For $s\leq t$, each lineage has marginal backward kernel $B^J$.
The diagonal terms in the pair difference vanish, so
\[
\sum_{k\ne l}B_u^J(j,k)B_u^J(j',l)\{f(k)-f(l)\}
=(B_u^Jf)(j)-(B_u^Jf)(j').
\]
Conditional on the revealed suffix through $t$, contraction bounds
the absolute conditional mean by
\[
\left|\E[\Delta_s\mid\mathcal S_t]\right|
\leq CG'(\theta)\varrho_*^{t-s}\mathbf1_{\{I_t\ne I'_t\}},
\]
where $\mathcal S_t$ contains that suffix and the clouds.
Multiplying by the suffix-measurable $\Delta_t$ gives
\begin{equation}
|\E[\Delta_s\Delta_t\mid\mathcal C_n]|
\leq CG'(\theta)^2\varrho_*^{t-s}\lambda^{n-t}.
\label{eq:logfree-pair}
\end{equation}
\endgraf
Under (A6), set $a_t=\max(1,t-r-1)$. For nonoverlapping blocks
($a_t>s$), reveal the later block down to $a_t$.
Let $\mathcal S_{a_t}$ contain the clouds, the two node labels at $a_t$,
and their revealed parent indices and simulator draws strictly after
$a_t$. Integrating the earlier block down from time
$s$ gives a function $f_s$ with $\|f_s\|_\infty\leq CG'\kappa^r$.
The marginal backward kernels give
\begin{align*}
\E[\Delta_s^{[r]}\mid\mathcal S_{a_t}]
&=(B_{a_t}^J\cdots B_{s+1}^Jf_s)(I_{a_t})
 -(B_{a_t}^J\cdots B_{s+1}^Jf_s)(I'_{a_t}),\\
\left|\E[\Delta_s^{[r]}\mid\mathcal S_{a_t}]\right|
&\leq CG'\kappa^r\varrho_*^{a_t-s}
                         \mathbf1_{\{I_{a_t}\ne I'_{a_t}\}}.
\end{align*}
Multiply by the $\mathcal S_{a_t}$-measurable $\Delta_t^{[r]}$, whose
absolute value is at most $CG'\kappa^r\mathbf1_{\{I_t\ne I'_t\}}$,
and use the survival bound. For overlapping blocks, use boundedness.
Together these give
\begin{equation}
|\E[\Delta_s^{[r]}\Delta_t^{[r]}\mid\mathcal C_n]|
\leq CG'(\theta)^2\kappa^{2r}
 \varrho_*^{(t-s-r-2)_+}\lambda^{n-t}.
\end{equation}
If the later block reaches the initial-history layer, the blocks overlap
and boundedness applies.
Expanding the square of the discounted sum and writing $q=n-t$,
$d=t-s$ gives
\begin{align}
&\E\left[\left|\sum_{s=1}^n\alpha^{n-s}\Delta_s^{[r]}\right|^2
       \middle|\mathcal C_n\right]\notag\\
&\quad\leq CG'(\theta)^2\kappa^{2r}
 \sum_{q=0}^{n-1}(\alpha^2\lambda)^q
 \left\{1+2\sum_{d\geq1}\alpha^d
                  \varrho_*^{(d-r-2)_+}\right\}\notag\\
&\quad\leq CG'(\theta)^2\kappa^{2r}(r+3)
                    \sum_{q=0}^{n-1}(\alpha^2\lambda)^q.
\end{align}
The weighted empirical variance is half this paired second moment.
Minkowski over ages costs the finite sum
$\sum_r\kappa^r\sqrt{r+3}$. Using \eqref{eq:logfree-transfer-identity},
\begin{align}
\E\|\nabla_\theta\hat\ell_J^{A,\alpha}
      -\nabla_\theta\hat\ell_J^{B,\alpha}\|_2^2
&\leq\frac{CpG'(\theta)^2}{J}
   \sum_{n=1}^N\sum_{q=0}^{n-1}
       \{\alpha^2(1-\epsilon^2/J)\}^q\notag\\
&\leq CpG'(\theta)^2\frac{N\min\{H_N(\alpha),J\}}{J}.
\label{eq:logfree-transfer}
\end{align}
The inner sum is bounded by both $H_N(\alpha)$ and $J/\epsilon^2$.
The $L^2$ triangle inequality with the after-score bound proves
\eqref{eq:review-alpha-particle-mse} for the before score. Equality of
expectations gives \eqref{eq:review-alpha-particle-bias}.
\endgraf
}


\end{proof}

\subsection{Full-score mean squared error}

\begin{thm}[MSE bounds for DMOP-$\alpha$]
\label{thm:refined-dmop-alpha-mse}
\kevin{\normalfont\normalsize Under the conditions of Lemmas~\ref{lem:dmop-alpha-variance} and
\ref{lem:dmop-population-discount-bias}, use either the before- or
after-resampling estimator and take
$\varrho=1-\underbar M/(2\bar M)\in[\tfrac12,1)$.
For $\alpha\in[0,1]$,
\begin{align}
&\E\|\nabla_\theta\hat\ell^\alpha(\theta)-\nabla_\theta\ell(\theta)\|_2^2
\notag\\*
&\quad\lesssim pG'(\theta)^2\Bigg[
\frac{N}{J}\min\left\{\sum_{r=0}^{N-1}\alpha^r,J\right\}
+\frac{N^2}{J^2}\notag\\*
&\qquad+
\frac{(1-\sqrt{2(1-\varrho)})^2(1-\alpha)^2}
 {(1+\sqrt{2(1-\varrho)})^2}
\left\{N-1+\sum_{q=1}^{N-2}(N-1-q)
\left(\alpha\frac{2\varrho-1}{3-2\varrho}\right)^q\right\}^2
\Bigg].
\label{eq:verified-dmop-alpha-mse-finite-sum}
\end{align}
The sum beginning at $q=1$ is empty for $N\leq2$.
For $0\leq\alpha<1$, a simpler bound is
\begin{align}
&\E\|\nabla_\theta\hat\ell^\alpha(\theta)-\nabla_\theta\ell(\theta)\|_2^2
\notag\\*
&\quad\lesssim pG'(\theta)^2\Bigg[
\frac{N}{J(1-\alpha)}+\frac{N^2}{J^2}\notag\\*
&\hspace{60pt}+N^2\left\{
\frac{1-\sqrt{2(1-\varrho)}}{1+\sqrt{2(1-\varrho)}}
\frac{(1-\alpha)(3-2\varrho)}
 {3-2\varrho-\alpha(2\varrho-1)}
\right\}^2\Bigg].
\label{eq:verified-dmop-alpha-mse-bound}
\end{align}
The endpoint bounds are
\begin{align}
&\E\|\nabla_\theta\hat\ell^0(\theta)-\nabla_\theta\ell(\theta)\|_2^2
\notag\\
&\quad\lesssim pG'(\theta)^2\left[
\frac NJ+\frac{N^2}{J^2}
+(N-1)^2\left\{
\frac{1-\sqrt{2(1-\varrho)}}{1+\sqrt{2(1-\varrho)}}
\right\}^2\right],
\label{eq:verified-dmop-zero-mse-bound}\\
&\E\|\nabla_\theta\hat\ell^1(\theta)-\nabla_\theta\ell(\theta)\|_2^2
\lesssim pG'(\theta)^2
\min\left\{\frac{N^2}{J},\,N+\frac{N^2}{J^2}\right\}.
\label{eq:verified-dmop-one-mse-bound}
\end{align}
At $\varrho=\tfrac12$ the population terms are zero. For every model,
the population discount bias is zero at $\alpha=1$.
Remark~\ref{rem:population-bias-cancellation} retains signed cancellation.}
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
\kevin{\normalfont\normalsize
Insert Equations~(\ref{eq:review-alpha-particle-mse}) and
(\ref{eq:population-discount-sharp-transition-bound}) into
Equation~(\ref{eq:generic-mse-particle-population-split}). This proves
Equation~(\ref{eq:verified-dmop-alpha-mse-finite-sum}). For $\alpha<1$,
\begin{align*}
\sum_{r=0}^{N-1}\alpha^r&\leq(1-\alpha)^{-1},\\
\sum_{q=0}^{N-2}(N-1-q)
 \left(\alpha\frac{2\varrho-1}{3-2\varrho}\right)^q
&\leq\frac{(N-1)(3-2\varrho)}{3-2\varrho-\alpha(2\varrho-1)}.
\end{align*}
These inequalities give Equation~(\ref{eq:verified-dmop-alpha-mse-bound}).
At $\alpha=0$, the memory sum is one and only $q=0$ remains in the
population sum. At $\alpha=1$, the population term vanishes and
\[
\frac{N\min\{N,J\}}J+\frac{N^2}{J^2}
\leq\min\left\{\frac{3N^2}{2J},\,N+\frac{N^2}{J^2}\right\},
\qquad J\geq2.
\]
This proves both endpoint bounds.}
\end{proof}

\begin{cor}[MSE averaged over data and particles]
\label{cor:data-averaged-full-mse}
\kevin{\normalfont\normalsize
Suppose the conditions of
Proposition~\ref{prop:dmop-alpha-data-averaged-score-discrepancy}
and Lemma~\ref{lem:dmop-alpha-variance} hold uniformly over the
observations, including either (A6) or (A6-J). Let
$H_N(\alpha)=\sum_{r=0}^{N-1}\alpha^r$. For either multinomial
estimator, evaluated at $\theta=\phi=\theta_0$,
\begin{align}
&\E_{\theta_0}\E_{\mathrm{PF}}\left[
\left\|\nabla_\theta\hat\ell_J^\alpha(\theta_0)
-\nabla_\theta\ell(\theta_0)\right\|_2^2\mid Y_{1:N}\right]
\notag\\
&\quad\lesssim pG'(\theta_0)^2
\left\{\frac{N\min\{H_N(\alpha),J\}}J
      +\frac{N^2}{J^2}+N(1-\alpha)^2\right\}.
\label{eq:data-averaged-full-mse}
\end{align}
Here the inner expectation is over the particle filter for the observed
data, and the outer expectation is over the data-generating law.}
\end{cor}

\begin{proof}
\kevin{\normalfont\normalsize
Average the particle/population decomposition in
Equation~(\ref{eq:generic-mse-particle-population-split}) over the data.
The uniform particle bound in
Equation~(\ref{eq:review-alpha-particle-mse}) controls its first term,
and Proposition~\ref{prop:dmop-alpha-data-averaged-score-discrepancy}
controls its second.}
\end{proof}

\kevin{\normalfont\normalsize
For $\alpha<1$, $H_N(\alpha)\leq(1-\alpha)^{-1}$. Balancing the
corresponding particle term with the population term gives
\begin{align}
\frac{N}{J(1-\alpha)}&\asymp N(1-\alpha)^2,
&1-\alpha&\asymp J^{-1/3},\notag\\
\E_{\theta_0}\E_{\mathrm{PF}}\left[
\left\|\nabla_\theta\hat\ell_J^\alpha-\nabla_\theta\ell\right\|_2^2
\mid Y_{1:N}\right]
&\lesssim pG'(\theta_0)^2
\left\{\frac{N}{J^{2/3}}+\frac{N^2}{J^2}\right\}.
\label{eq:data-averaged-alpha-tuning}
\end{align}
This deterministic choice gives an attainable bound. The endpoint
$\alpha=1$ retains the bound in
Equation~(\ref{eq:verified-dmop-one-mse-bound}) and can give the smaller
bound for short observation sequences.}

\begin{remark}[Neutral-model check]
To check the rates in Lemma~\ref{lem:dmop-alpha-variance}, \kevin{\normalfont\normalsize for the after-resampling estimator,} take
iid ${\rm Uniform}[0,1]$ states,
constant weights at $\theta=\phi=0$, and
\begin{equation}
\Pr_\theta(Y_n=1\mid X_n=x)
=\frac12+\frac\theta4(2x-1),
\qquad y_n^*=1.
\end{equation}
Then $h_n=(2X_n-1)/2$ has variance $\sigma^2=1/12$, and direct
conditional multinomial calculation gives
\begin{equation}
\Var\left\{\nabla_\theta\hat\ell^\alpha(\theta)\right\}
=
\frac{\sigma^2}{J}
\left[
N(2-J^{-1})
+\sum_{r=1}^{N-1}(N-r)\alpha^{2r}(1-J^{-1})^{r+1}
\right].
\label{eq:neutral-dmop-alpha-exact-variance}
\end{equation}
\kevin{\normalfont\normalsize For fixed $\alpha<1$, the variance is of order $N/J$. Its
genealogical contribution has order $N/\{J(1-\alpha^2)\}$ as
$\alpha$ approaches one, before horizon or coalescence saturation, consistent with the linear memory
factor in Equation~(\ref{eq:review-alpha-particle-mse}).}
At $\alpha=1$ and $J/N\to\infty$, the same exact formula gives
\begin{equation}
\Var\left\{\nabla_\theta\hat\ell^1(\theta)\right\}
\sim\frac{\sigma^2}{J}
\left\{2N+\sum_{r=1}^{N-1}(N-r)\right\}
=\frac{\sigma^2N(N+3)}{2J}.
\end{equation}
\kevin{\normalfont\normalsize The full-score endpoint bound in
Equation~(\ref{eq:verified-dmop-one-mse-bound}) matches this order
when $J/N\to\infty$. This example does not establish sharpness of
every term in the general bound.}
\end{remark}


\begin{remark}[Rates and normalization]
\kevin{\normalfont\normalsize Theorem~\ref{thm:refined-dmop-alpha-mse} bounds error relative to
the full likelihood score. DMOP-$0$ has the upper bound
\[
pG'(\theta)^2\left[\frac NJ+\frac{N^2}{J^2}
+(N-1)^2\left\{\frac{1-\sqrt{2(1-\varrho)}}
 {1+\sqrt{2(1-\varrho)}}\right\}^2\right].
\]
Thus its particle error can decrease with $J$ while its full-score
MSE retains population discount bias. For fixed $0<\alpha<1$, the
corresponding population term is of order at most $N^2(1-\alpha)^2$
with fixed model constants. At $\alpha=1$ it is zero, and
Equation~(\ref{eq:verified-dmop-one-mse-bound}) gives the full-score
upper-bound orders
\[
\begin{cases}
N^2/J^2,&2\leq J\leq\sqrt N,\\
N,&\sqrt N<J<N,\\
N^2/J,&J\geq N,
\end{cases}
\]
suppressing $pG'(\theta)^2$. The ratio of $N+N^2/J^2$ to $N^2/J$
is $J/N+1/J$, so there is an order improvement when $J\to\infty$ and
$J/N\to0$.
\endgraf
A particle MSE bound for $\operatorname{osc}(f)\leq1$ is multiplied by
$\operatorname{osc}(f)^2$ when applied to an unnormalized functional.
A generic $N^2/J$ bound applied once to a score of oscillation $O(N)$
therefore gives $N^4/J$. Here the proof bounds the individual
measurement-gradient contributions and sums their covariances, so
there is no additional $N^2$ normalization factor. The finite-$J$
MSE theorem is distinct from the fixed-horizon CLT and quadratic
asymptotic-variance lower growth of \citet{poyiadjis11}.}
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}[Choosing $\alpha$ in the upper bound]
\label{rem:alpha-tuning-upper-bound}
\kevin{\normalfont\normalsize Take $N\geq2$ and fixed $\tfrac12<\varrho<1$. The explicit
population term in Equation~(\ref{eq:verified-dmop-alpha-mse-bound})
is bounded above and below by positive model constants times
$N^2(1-\alpha)^2$. Before the finite memory reaches its cap, balancing
it with the particle term gives
\begin{align}
\frac{N}{J(1-\alpha)}&\asymp N^2(1-\alpha)^2,\notag\\
1-\alpha&\asymp(NJ)^{-1/3},\notag\\
\E\|\nabla_\theta\hat\ell^\alpha-\nabla_\theta\ell\|_2^2
&\lesssim pG'(\theta)^2
\left\{\frac{N^{4/3}}{J^{2/3}}+\frac{N^2}{J^2}\right\}.
\label{eq:alpha-tuning-varrho-balance}
\end{align}
Comparing with the improved endpoint gives the following attainable
full-score bounds:
\begin{equation}
\begin{array}{c|c|c}
\text{regime}&1-\alpha&
\text{MSE upper-bound order}/\{pG'(\theta)^2\}\\\hline
2\leq J\leq\sqrt N&0&N^2/J^2\\
\sqrt N<J<N^2&\asymp(NJ)^{-1/3}&N^{4/3}/J^{2/3}\\
J\geq N^2&0&N^2/J.
\end{array}
\label{eq:alpha-tuning-soft-lag-regimes}
\end{equation}
Indeed, $N^{4/3}/J^{2/3}$ and $N^2/J^2$ agree at $J=\sqrt N$,
while $N^{4/3}/J^{2/3}$ and $N^2/J$ agree at $J=N^2$.
For $J\leq\sqrt N$, any choice $0\leq1-\alpha\leq J^{-1}$ also
attains the first order: the particle term is at most $N$, and the
population term is at most a constant times $N^2/J^2$.
The endpoint is therefore a rate-achieving choice, not a claim that
$\alpha=1$ is optimal.
\endgraf
The ratios of the DMOP-$1$ upper-bound order to the middle-row order are
\[
\begin{cases}
(J^2/N)^{1/3},&\sqrt N<J\leq N,\\
(N^2/J)^{1/3},&N\leq J<N^2.
\end{cases}
\]
At $J=N$, the bounds are $N$ and $N^{2/3}$. These are comparisons
between upper bounds, not lower bounds or actual-MSE dominance.
\endgraf
The finite particle term is capped when
$\sum_{r=0}^{N-1}\alpha^r\geq J$. Thus, when $J<N$, it is locally
constant near $\alpha=1$ and does not supply a strict endpoint
improvement from discounting. When $J\geq N$, its left derivative at
one is $N^2(N-1)/(2J)>0$, while the squared population term has
derivative zero. A small decrease from one then reduces this
particular upper bound, though it need not change its order or the
actual MSE.
\endgraf
At $\varrho=\tfrac12$, the population term vanishes and $\alpha=0$
attains $pG'(\theta)^2(N/J+N^2/J^2)$. For $N=1$, all choices give
the same estimator. If the signed first-order population term in
Remark~\ref{rem:population-bias-cancellation} vanishes, its squared
bound is instead $O(N^2(1-\alpha)^4)$. The same balance then gives
\begin{align}
1-\alpha&\asymp(NJ)^{-1/5},\notag\\
\E\|\nabla_\theta\hat\ell^\alpha-\nabla_\theta\ell\|_2^2
&\lesssim pG'(\theta)^2
\left\{\frac{N^{6/5}}{J^{4/5}}+\frac{N^2}{J^2}\right\},
\label{eq:alpha-tuning-signed-cancellation}
\end{align}
again compared with the endpoint bound. This last improvement requires
the stated cancellation condition.}
\end{remark}



\begin{remark}[Geometric discounting and lag truncation]
\kevin{\normalfont\normalsize For $0\leq k<N$, define the after-resampling lag truncation and its
population counterpart by
\begin{align}
\nabla_\theta\hat s_k^1&=\sum_{n=1}^N
 \sum_{r=0}^{\min(k,n-1)}\Delta_{n,r}^J,&
\nabla_\theta s_k^1&=\sum_{n=1}^N
 \sum_{r=0}^{\min(k,n-1)}\Delta_{n,r}.
\label{eq:fixed-lag-score-definition-s13}
\end{align}
Setting $b_s=\min(s+k,N)$, the finite telescope is
\[
\nabla_\theta\hat s_k^1
=\sum_{s=1}^{N-k-1}m(\xi_{s+k})(h_s)
+m(\xi_N)\left(\sum_{s=N-k}^Nh_s\right).
\]
In the direct particle-MSE proof, replace each creation time's
geometric endpoint weights by unit mass at $b_s$. Their total mass is
one and $b_s-s+1\leq k+1$. The predictable local-error sum and ratio
remainder are unchanged. The descendant-weight calculation replaces
$H_N(\alpha)$ by $k+1$. Hence
\begin{align}
\E\|\nabla_\theta\hat s_k^1-\nabla_\theta s_k^1\|_2^2
&\lesssim pG'(\theta)^2
\left\{\frac{N\min\{k+1,J\}}J+\frac{N^2}{J^2}\right\},\notag\\
\|\E\nabla_\theta\hat s_k^1-\nabla_\theta s_k^1\|_2
&\lesssim N\sqrt p\,G'(\theta)/J.
\label{eq:review-truncated-score-bounds}
\end{align}
\endgraf
For $\tfrac12<\varrho<1$, Equation~
(\ref{eq:population-discount-vector-boundary-comparison}) gives
\[
\|\nabla_\theta\ell-\nabla_\theta s_k^1\|_2
\leq2(N-k-1)\sqrt p\,G'(\theta)
\frac{1-\sqrt{2(1-\varrho)}}{1+\sqrt{2(1-\varrho)}}
\left(\frac{2\varrho-1}{3-2\varrho}\right)^k.
\]
Combining these two bounds yields the full-score MSE bound
\begin{align}
&\E\|\nabla_\theta\hat s_k^1-\nabla_\theta\ell\|_2^2\notag\\
&\quad\lesssim pG'(\theta)^2\Bigg[
\frac{N\min\{k+1,J\}}J+\frac{N^2}{J^2}\notag\\
&\qquad+(N-k-1)^2
\left\{\frac{1-\sqrt{2(1-\varrho)}}{1+\sqrt{2(1-\varrho)}}\right\}^2
\left(\frac{2\varrho-1}{3-2\varrho}\right)^{2k}
\Bigg].
\end{align}
The population error is zero at $k=N-1$, and at $\varrho=\tfrac12$
for every $k$. Put $\beta_k=(1-\alpha)\alpha^k$ for $k<N-1$ and
$\beta_{N-1}=\alpha^{N-1}$. Since $\sum_{k=r}^{N-1}\beta_k=\alpha^r$,
\begin{equation}
\nabla_\theta\hat\ell^\alpha=\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}\beta_k\nabla_\theta\hat s_k^1,
\qquad \sum_{k=0}^{N-1}\beta_k(k+1)=\sum_{r=0}^{N-1}\alpha^r.
\label{eq:dmop-alpha-fixed-lag-mixture-s13}
\end{equation}
This relation gives comparable particle bounds for comparable memory.
It does not equate their full-score MSE bounds, because geometric
discounting also changes the coefficients of recent lags.}
\end{remark}



\section{Implementation and Figures for Bayesian Inference}
\label{appendix:bayes}

\subsection{A common sampling target}
\label{sec:bayes-common-target}

For a comparison with a common posterior, we implemented NUTS and particle
Metropolis--Hastings (PMMH) using the Dhaka model in Pypomp.
The process simulator uses Gaussian noise with 20 Euler steps per monthly
observation. We fix the initial state proportions at the library values,
with $\rho=0$, $c=1$, $\alpha=1$ and $\delta=0.02$.
Here $\alpha$ is a model parameter, distinct from the DMOP discount parameter.
The observation distribution is Gaussian, with mean equal to the simulated
monthly deaths and standard deviation equal to $\tau$ times that mean.
We omit the additive measurement-density floor used in the library's
optimization code. Paths that leave the nonnegative state space are assigned
zero likelihood for the observed series.

Let $x$ denote the 18 estimation coordinates. These comprise the logarithms
of the five positive parameters $\gamma,m,\epsilon,\sigma,\tau$, the trend
coordinate $100\beta_{\mathrm{trend}}$, and the six coefficients in each
seasonal function. The unused constant environmental-transmission parameter
in the earlier implementation is excluded.
We use independent Gaussian priors for these coordinates, common to all
chains and both methods. For each coordinate, the mean is the midpoint
of the interval in Table~\ref{tab:bayes-prior}, and the standard deviation
is half its width. These intervals come from the parameter search ranges;
they do not truncate the Gaussian distributions.
Thus, writing $a_i$ and $b_i$ for the endpoints,
\[
 z_i=\frac{x_i-(a_i+b_i)/2}{(b_i-a_i)/2},
 \qquad p(z)=\prod_{i=1}^{18}\phi(z_i;0,1).
\]
This specifies a reference prior without fitting a density to IF2 particles.
The prior density is evaluated in $z$, so the sampling calculation requires
no further Jacobian factor. On the natural parameter scale, the priors for
the five positive parameters are lognormal.

\begin{table}[htbp]
\centering
\begin{tabular}{lll}
\toprule
Estimation coordinate & $a_i$ & $b_i$ \\
\midrule
$\log\gamma$ & $\log 10$ & $\log 40$ \\
$\log m$ & $\log 0.03$ & $\log 0.6$ \\
$\log\epsilon$ & $\log 0.2$ & $\log 30$ \\
$100\beta_{\mathrm{trend}}$ & $-1$ & $0$ \\
$\log\sigma$ & $\log 1$ & $\log 5$ \\
$\log\tau$ & $\log 0.1$ & $\log 0.5$ \\
Seasonal transmission coefficients 1 and 3 & $-4$ & $4$ \\
Seasonal transmission coefficients 2, 4, 5 and 6 & $0$ & $8$ \\
Each seasonal environmental-transmission coefficient & $-10$ & $0$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\caption{Intervals specifying the means and standard deviations of the
common Gaussian prior on the estimation scale. A sensitivity calculation
doubles all prior standard deviations, keeping the means fixed.}
\label{tab:bayes-prior}
\end{table}

Let $u$ denote the particle-filter random numbers, with law $m$, and let
$\widehat L_J(z,u)$ be the bootstrap particle-filter likelihood estimate.
The augmented target is
\begin{equation}
 \widetilde\pi(z,u)\ \propto\ \widehat L_J(z,u)\,p(z)\,m(u).
 \label{eq:bayes-common-target}
\end{equation}
Unbiasedness of the likelihood estimate gives the posterior for the
specified Euler model as the marginal distribution of $z$ \citep{andrieu10}.
The log likelihood and log prior enter without division by the number of
observations. We use $J=10,000$ particles and double precision for this
likelihood calculation in both methods.

PMMH jointly proposes $z'=z+hB\xi$, where $\xi\sim N(0,I)$, and independent
random numbers $u'\sim m$. Its acceptance probability is
\[
 1\wedge\frac{\widehat L_J(z',u')p(z')}{\widehat L_J(z,u)p(z)}.
\]
On rejection, both $z$ and $u$ are retained, together with the cached
likelihood estimate. The matrix $BB^T$ is estimated from pilot PMMH draws
and held fixed; the scalar $h$ is adapted during 500 warmup iterations.
Preparation of the starting points and proposal scales includes numerical
optimization with derivatives, although the PMMH transition itself
requires only process simulation and measurement-density evaluation.

NUTS first proposes independent random numbers $u'\sim m$, accepting with
probability $1\wedge\widehat L_J(z,u')/\widehat L_J(z,u)$, and then updates
$z$ conditional on the retained $u$.
We use BlackJAX's NUTS trajectory construction, with maximum tree depth 5.
DMOP with discount parameter 0.95 and 1,000 particles supplies the proposal
force, evaluated in single precision with deterministic GPU operations.
Every trajectory weight uses the double-precision likelihood in
Equation~\ref{eq:bayes-common-target} and the common prior.
The force and likelihood are therefore separate numerical calculations.
Step sizes are adapted during 500 warmup iterations and then held fixed.
The implementation, prior specification and checks of the sampling kernels
are available in \texttt{code/bayesian/corrected}.

\subsection{\sout{Archived sampling calculation and empirical priors}\kevin{Sampling settings and diagnostics}}

\sout{The code and four saved chains underlying Figure~\refFigNutsEb\ are
available in \texttt{code/bayesian}, together with the script producing
Table~\mbox{\ref{tab:bayesian-diagnostics}} and their original \texttt{diffPomp}
source revision.
This earlier calculation fixes the initial state proportions and uses
19 finite transformed parameter coordinates, denoted by \mbox{$z$}.
Positive rates and noise scales are represented by their logarithms,
the trend coordinate is \mbox{$100\beta_{\mathrm{trend}}$}, and the seasonal
coefficients are unchanged. The two remaining stored coordinates fix
\mbox{$\rho=0$} and \mbox{$c=1$}. An additional coordinate \mbox{$\log\omega$} is retained
in the fitted prior although the seasonal environmental force of infection
replaces this constant in the simulator.}

\sout{For each chain \mbox{$c$}, we ran 40 IF2 iterations with 10,000 particles from
a random starting point. The perturbation standard deviation was initially
0.02 on the transformed scale and decreased by a factor of 0.95 per iteration.
We selected the swarm with the highest monitored likelihood and initialized
NUTS at its mean. If its particles are \mbox{$z_{c,1},\ldots,z_{c,K}$}, with
\mbox{$K=10,000$}, the empirical prior was the joint Gaussian kernel estimate}

\[\text{\sout{\mbox{$\displaystyle \widehat\pi_c(z)=\frac{1}{K}\sum_{k=1}^K
 \phi_{19}(z;z_{c,k},h^2 S_c),\qquad
 h=K^{-1/(19+4)}\simeq0.670,$}}}\]

\sout{where \mbox{$S_c$} is the sample covariance of the swarm. This is the default
Scott bandwidth used by \texttt{jax.scipy.stats.gaussian\_kde}.
The prior is defined on the transformed coordinates, so no additional
change-of-variables factor enters the calculation in \mbox{$z$}.
Each chain has its own fitted prior. The observations are therefore used
both to construct that prior and to evaluate the likelihood, with the
fitted density subsequently held fixed.}

\sout{The numerical log potential supplied to NUTS was}

\[\text{\sout{\mbox{$\displaystyle a_c(z;u)=\frac{\widehat\ell_J(z;u)+\log\widehat\pi_c(z)}{N},
 \qquad N=600,\quad J=1,000,$}}}\]

\sout{where \mbox{$\widehat\ell_J$} is the forward particle-filter log objective
returned by the MOP routine, including its measurement-density stabilization,
and \mbox{$u$} denotes the simulation random numbers.
The archived sampling code uses \mbox{$\alpha=0.95$} for its DMOP derivative.
Thus the supplied potential corresponds numerically to a tempered density
proportional to \mbox{$\{\exp(\widehat\ell_J)\widehat\pi_c\}^{1/N}$},
rather than the untempered empirical-prior posterior.
The resampling genealogy is recalculated at each candidate parameter;
the stop-gradient operations freeze the resampling measurements only
for differentiation. The code draws its simulation seed and systematic
resampling offsets using NumPy during JAX tracing, so these random
numbers are reused by the compiled computation. They are not part of
an augmented Markov-chain state, and no separate pseudo-marginal update
is applied. This construction does not establish exact posterior sampling.}

\sout{We used TensorFlow Probability's JAX \texttt{NoUTurnSampler}, with step
size 0.05, maximum tree depth 5, and maximum energy difference 1,000.
There was no step-size adaptation. Each run saved 1,500 draws; the figures
discard the first 500. The library's NUTS transition uses the supplied
potential and its AD force, without an additional likelihood correction.
Only the parameter draws were saved. The fitted prior clouds, kernel
diagnostic traces and computation times were not archived, so prior
overlays, divergence counts, tree-depth saturation and ESS per second
cannot be recovered from these files.}

\sout{Table~\mbox{\ref{tab:bayesian-diagnostics}} summarizes the retained draws.
The between-chain differences include differences in fitted priors, so
\mbox{$\widehat R$} here describes disagreement between runs and cannot establish
convergence to a common posterior. We calculate ESS and MCSE separately
within each chain; these estimates also require approximate stationarity.}

\kevin{We ran four chains of each method from the same four starting points,
obtained by optimization followed by a pilot PMMH run.
Figure~\refFigNutsEb\ compares the first 2,000 retained iterations of each
chain, after 500 warmup iterations. A longer PMMH run provides 30,000
retained iterations per chain for comparison of the marginal distributions
(Figure~\ref{fig:mh}). No draws from the earlier tempered calculation enter
these figures.}

\kevin{Both methods use fixed covariance matrices estimated from preliminary
PMMH runs. The PMMH covariance uses 1\% diagonal shrinkage and a $10^{-6}I$ ridge.
For NUTS, we combine the pooled covariance from the first 5,000 retained PMMH
iterations per chain with this matrix, with weights 0.75 and 0.25, and rescale
to the trace of the PMMH proposal covariance. These matrices affect the proposals but not the prior.
Only the scalar step size is adapted during warmup, with target acceptance
probabilities 0.65 for NUTS and 0.234 for PMMH. Initial step sizes are 0.07
and 0.5, respectively. The final NUTS step sizes range from 0.048 to 0.060.
The proposal matrices, starting points, random seeds and software versions
are saved with the code in \texttt{code/bayesian/corrected}.}

\kevin{Table~\ref{tab:bayesian-diagnostics} gives diagnostics for all 18
coordinates in the 2,000-iteration comparison. For the trend coordinate,
NUTS has $\widehat R=1.014$ and bulk ESS 333, compared with 1.025 and 73 for
PMMH. The largest NUTS $\widehat R$ is 1.059, and the smallest bulk ESS is
60, both for the first seasonal environmental-transmission coefficient.
There are two divergent NUTS transitions among 8,000 retained iterations.
Almost all trajectories reach the depth limit of 5, corresponding to
31 integration steps. Thus the traces show improved exploration without
establishing convergence of all coordinates.}

\kevin{The longer PMMH runs give broadly similar marginal distributions
(Figure~\ref{fig:mh}); their largest $\widehat R$ is 1.014 and smallest bulk
ESS is 202. Doubling all prior standard deviations has little effect on
the sampled trend standard deviation (0.112 versus 0.115), but gives wider
lower tails for some seasonal environmental-transmission coefficients.
These tails also mix slowly, so the comparison does not establish prior
insensitivity. The prior overlays in Figure~\ref{fig:posteriors} make this
issue visible. The calculations were run on shared GPUs, and preparation
of both samplers used optimization and pilot runs. The iteration comparison
therefore does not measure a computing-time speedup.}

\begin{table}[htbp]
\centering
\resizebox{\textwidth}{!}{\kevin{\input{code/bayesian/corrected/results/paper/diagnostics}}}
\caption{\sout{Diagnostics for the four archived empirical-prior NUTS runs,
using 1,000 retained draws per run. The rank-normalized split
\mbox{$\widehat R$} compares all four runs, whose fitted priors differ.
Bulk and tail ESS are the smallest within-chain values, and MCSE is
the largest within-chain Monte~Carlo standard error of the mean.
All quantities use the displayed transformed coordinates. The fixed
coordinates \mbox{$\rho=0$} and \mbox{$c=1$} are excluded. Divergences and tree-depth
diagnostics were not saved.}\kevin{Diagnostics for four chains of each method, using 2,000 retained iterations per chain after 500 warmup iterations. Rank-normalized split $\widehat R$, bulk and tail ESS, and the Monte Carlo standard error of the mean (MCSE) are calculated across chains on the displayed estimation coordinates. All chains have the common prior and target specified above. Bold entries indicate $\widehat R$ closer to 1, larger ESS, or smaller MCSE.}}

\section{Algorithmic Parameters}
\label{appendix:algparams}

We include the full set of algorithmic parameters used for the Dhaka results in Table~\ref{table:algparams}. 
The warm start iterations are IF2 iterations, which are followed by gradient descent (GD) iterations when using IFAD. 
The IF2 cooling rate is the fractional reduction in the parameter perturbations over 50 iterations, so that a cooling rate of 0.5 corresponds to approximately 1\% cooling per iteration.
The random walk standard deviation (RWSD) scales the noise added at each filtering step to each regular parameter.
Initial value parameters (IVPs), that affect the value of the latent process only at time $t_0$, are treated differently: they are perturbed only at time $t_0$, with a larger RWSD. 
Parameters are transformed to a unit scale, for example by taking logarithms or a logistic transformation, so that a single RWSD value works for all parameters.

\label{table:algparams}
\end{table}

Following some experimentation, IFAD was implemnenteed by plugging the DMOP gradient estimate into the Adam optimizer \citep{kingma15}.
The momentum parameter in Adam was turned off for IFAD-0, since this low-variance estimate did not reqquire it.
Decreasing the learning rate (LR) was found to be helpful, and we selected the commonly used cosine decay.
Warm-up steps were taken with Adam to stabilize its initial performance and avoid noisy

\section{Application to a Daphnia Population Model}
\label{sec:daphnia}

\subsection{Model and data}

The SIRJPF2 model developed by \citet{yang25daphnia} describes interactions between two species of \textit{Daphnia}, their algal food, and a fungal parasite.
We use the version with unit-specific infected-adult mortality to compare likelihood maximization by IFAD and the marginalized panel iterated filter (MPIF) of \citet{wheeler25}.

The data come from the mesocosm experiment of \citet{searle16}.
We use the treatment containing the native species \textit{D.~dentifera}, the invasive species \textit{D.~lumholtzi}, and the parasite \textit{Metschnikowia bicuspidata}, with eight mesocosms, each treated as one unit in the panel.
Each mesocosm was observed at ten times, $t_n=5n+2$ days for $n=1,\ldots,10$.
At each observation time, a one-liter sample was taken and susceptible and infected adult females of both species were counted.
Juvenile counts are excluded from the likelihood.

We write $S_u^a(t)$, $I_u^a(t)$, and $J_u^a(t)$ for the densities of susceptible adult females, infected adult females, and juveniles, where $u=1,\ldots,8$ indexes the mesocosm and $a\in\{\mathrm n,\mathrm i\}$ denotes the native or invasive species.
The densities of food and parasite spores are $F_u(t)$ and $P_u(t)$.
These variables form an eight-dimensional latent state in each mesocosm.
Removal by death, consumption, or sampling is denoted by $R$ in the model name and does not require an additional state variable.
The two host species interact through their shared food and parasite populations.
The processes in different mesocosms are independent conditional on the parameters.

As in \citet{yang25daphnia}, the transition dynamics follow an It\^o stochastic differential equation (SDE):
\begin{align*}
dS_u^a
 &= \{\lambda_J J_u^a-(\theta_S^a+p^a f_S^aP_u+\delta)S_u^a\}\,dt
    +\sigma_S^a S_u^a\,dB_{S,u}^a,\\
dI_u^a
 &= \{p^a f_S^a S_u^aP_u-(\theta_{I,u}^a+\delta)I_u^a\}\,dt
    +\sigma_I^a I_u^a\,dB_{I,u}^a,\\
dJ_u^a
 &= \{r^a f_S^aF_uS_u^a-(\theta_J^a+\delta+\lambda_J)J_u^a\}\,dt
    +\sigma_J^a J_u^a\,dB_{J,u}^a,\\
dP_u
 &= \left\{\sum_a\left[\beta\theta_{I,u}^a I_u^a
          -f_S^a(S_u^a+\xi I_u^a)P_u\right]
          -(\theta_P+\delta)P_u\right\}\,dt
    +\sigma_P P_u\,dB_{P,u},\\
dF_u
 &= \left\{\mu-\delta F_u
          -\sum_a f_S^aF_u(S_u^a+\xi I_u^a+J_u^a)\right\}\,dt
    +\sigma_F F_u\,dB_{F,u},
\end{align*}
with independent standard Brownian motions $B$.
The time argument is suppressed in the equations.
Except for infected-adult mortality, estimated parameters are shared across mesocosms.
The parameters $r^a$ and $f_S^a$ are the reproductive rate and host filtration rate, $p^a$ controls infection following spore ingestion, and $\xi$ is the filtration rate of infected adults relative to susceptible adults.
The mortality or loss rates of susceptible adults, infected adults, juveniles, and spores are $\theta_S^a$, $\theta_{I,u}^a$, $\theta_J^a$, and $\theta_P$, respectively; the $\sigma$ parameters are process-noise coefficients.
The relative filtration rate of juveniles is fixed at one.

We use the units of the public Python implementation:\footnote{\href{https://github.com/pypomp/Daphnia-tutorial/blob/f3183301273a409e5b079bb0ae3a22246f69ba7a/Python-code/daphnia_tut_pypomp_advanced.qmd}{\texttt{pypomp/Daphnia-tutorial}, advanced tutorial, revision \texttt{f318330}.}}
\textit{Daphnia} densities are individuals per liter, $F$ is in $10^6$ algal cells per liter, and $P$ is in $10^3$ spores per liter, with parameters expressed in the corresponding units.
The sampling loss rate is fixed at $\delta=0.013$ per day, the food replenishment rate at $\mu=0.37$, the juvenile maturation rate at $\lambda_J=0.1$ per day, and the spore yield per infected-host death at $\beta=30$, corresponding to $3\times10^4$ spores.
The initial states are $S_u^{\mathrm n}=2.333$, $S_u^{\mathrm i}=0.667$, and $F_u=16.667$, with the remaining states set to zero.
Simulation begins at $t_0=1$ day, and parasites are introduced on day~4 at a density of $25\times10^3$ spores per liter.
We numerically solve the SDE using the Euler-Maruyama method with a time step of 0.25 days, following the initial conditions, inoculation, and numerical boundary rules of the public implementation.

The measurement model for the adult counts is
\[
Y_{S,u,n}^a\sim\operatorname{NBinomial}(S_u^a(t_n),\tau_S^a),
\qquad
Y_{I,u,n}^a\sim\operatorname{NBinomial}(I_u^a(t_n),\tau_I^a),
\]
where $\operatorname{NBinomial}(m,\tau)$ has mean $m$ and variance $m+m^2/\tau$ \citep{yang25daphnia}.
The four counts at each observation time are independent conditional on the latent state.
Since the sampled volume is one liter, the state densities and observed counts are on the same scale.
The numerical implementation sets a lower bound of $10^{-10}$ on the negative binomial mean and size parameters.
As in the public Python code, a state variable that crosses a numerical boundary is reset to zero, and that particle's log weight for the mesocosm at the next observation is set to $-150$.
The boundary thresholds and the treatment of the inoculation time are specified in the code.

We fix $\sigma_S^{\mathrm n}=\sigma_S^{\mathrm i}=0$ and estimate $\theta_{I,u}^{\mathrm n}$ and $\theta_{I,u}^{\mathrm i}$ separately for each mesocosm.
The remaining 22 estimated parameters are shared across mesocosms, giving $22+2\times8=38$ estimated parameters.
This is the model with unit-specific infected-adult mortality in Table~S3 of the archived revision \citep{yang26daphniacode}.

\subsection{Experimental settings}

We tested IFAD with discount factors $\alpha=0$, $\alpha=0.97$, and $\alpha=1$ against MPIF using Pypomp 1.0.5 \citep{abkemeier25}.
For each method, we performed 50 searches from the same starting parameter vectors, generated by independent lognormal perturbations of the initial parameter vector in the public tutorial, with a standard deviation of 0.45 on the log scale.
All positive estimated parameters were optimized on the log scale.
Each IFAD search used an MPIF warm start of 200 iterations followed by 200 iterations of the Adam optimizer \citep{kingma15}, using DMOP-$\alpha$ gradient estimates.
We ran MPIF alone for 680 iterations, chosen to approximate the computing time of IFAD; measured times are reported in Table~\ref{tab:daphnia-results}.

We used 1,000 particles per mesocosm during optimization.
The initial random walk standard deviation (RWSD) for MPIF was 0.05 on the log scale, with geometric cooling by a factor of 0.7 over 50 iterations.
Adam used $\beta_1=0.9$, $\beta_2=0.999$, and $\epsilon=10^{-8}$, with all eight mesocosms contributing to each update.
The learning-rate schedule used ten steps of linear warmup multiplied by cosine decay with a final factor of 0.1.
A summary of the algorithmic parameters is provided in Table~\ref{tab:daphnia-settings}.

The learning-rate scale was selected using four additional starting points, separate from the 50 used for the comparison.
We compared the six combinations of learning-rate scales $0.001$, $0.005$, and $0.01$ with $\alpha\in\{0.9,0.97\}$.
The combination with the highest median final log-likelihood, evaluated by independent particle filtering, was $0.01$ with $\alpha=0.97$.
We used the same learning-rate schedule for $\alpha=0$ and $\alpha=1$.

\centering
\begin{tabular}{p{4.5cm}cccc}
\toprule
\textbf{Hyperparameter} & \textbf{MPIF} & \textbf{IFAD-0} & \textbf{IFAD-0.97} & \textbf{IFAD-1} \\
\midrule
Particles per mesocosm ($J$) & 1,000 & 1,000 & 1,000 & 1,000 \\
MPIF Iterations & 680 & 200 & 200 & 200 \\
Adam Iterations & --- & 200 & 200 & 200 \\
MPIF Cooling Factor & 0.7 & 0.7 & 0.7 & 0.7 \\
Initial RWSD (log scale) & 0.05 & 0.05 & 0.05 & 0.05 \\

\label{tab:daphnia-settings}

All reported log-likelihoods were evaluated independently of optimization using ordinary particle filters, with 2,000 particles per mesocosm and ten replicates.
Likelihood estimates were averaged within each mesocosm before taking logarithms, and the eight mesocosm log-likelihoods were then summed.
Each method's best parameter vector was then held fixed and re-evaluated with ten fresh replicates at the same particle count, using the same model and Pypomp version on a CPU.
The same evaluation method was used for the final results and for intermediate saved parameter vectors.
The progress plot uses 25, 27, 26, and 22 saved parameter vectors per search for IFAD-0, IFAD-0.97, IFAD-1, and MPIF, respectively, including the final evaluations.
Time is assigned in proportion to iteration number within each stage, using the measured total optimization time for that stage.
Each set of 50 searches ran on one Tesla V100 16 GB GPU, with two searches per batch.
Optimization times are summed over the 25 batches and include initial compilation but exclude the additional particle filter evaluations.

\subsection{Results}

Our findings are summarized in Table~\ref{tab:daphnia-results} and Figures~\ref{fig:daphnia-progress} and~\ref{fig:daphnia-raincloud}.
IFAD-0.97 and IFAD-1 attained maximum log-likelihoods of $-858.48$ and $-858.27$, respectively, each with a Monte Carlo standard error (MCSE) of approximately 0.17.
The maximum for MPIF was $-860.06$, with MCSE 0.16.
IFAD-0 attained $-860.36$ and had a median log-likelihood close to that of MPIF.

\begin{table}[htbp!]
\centering
\input{imgs/daphnia/daphnia_table.tex}
\caption{Log-likelihoods from 50 searches per method, with MCSEs in parentheses. Re-evaluated uses fresh particle filters at each method's original maximizing parameter vector.}
\label{tab:daphnia-results}
\end{table}

Comparing searches from the same starting points, IFAD-0.97 and IFAD-1 each attained a higher final log-likelihood than MPIF in 49 of the 50 searches.
The median paired differences were 2.86 and 2.79 log-likelihood units, respectively.
The distributions of the final log-likelihoods for IFAD-0.97 and IFAD-1 were similar, which may be due to the short time series in each mesocosm.

\citet{yang26daphniacode} report a maximum log-likelihood of $-863.03$ in the revised Table~S3.
Our MPIF searches attained a higher value, and IFAD-0.97 and IFAD-1 improved on this further.

\section{Comparison with Other Methods for the Dhaka Model}
\label{sec:dhaka-competitors}

We compared IFAD with the transition-density method of \citet{ditlevsen19}
(DS19) and the differentiable particle filter of \citet{corenflos21}
(CTDD21) on the Dhaka
cholera model. We tested whether these methods could find high-likelihood
parameter estimates from the wide range of starting points used in the
main text, and whether they could improve estimates obtained by IF2.
We describe our implementations below.

\subsection{Methods}

\paragraph{DS19.}
The method of \citet{ditlevsen19} combines an approximate transition density
with particle smoothing and stochastic approximation expectation
maximization (SAEM). Their construction assumes that one smooth state
coordinate is observed directly and that noise acts directly on the
remaining coordinates. Applying it to Dhaka requires some modifications:
the six state coordinates $(S,I,R^1,R^2,R^3,M)$ are driven by one Brownian
motion, and the monthly death counts are observed with measurement error.

The SAEM objective includes the process log-density, which DS19 must
therefore be able to evaluate. Replacing the
SAEM maximization with a score update retains this requirement: the score
includes derivatives of the process log-density, evaluated along the
imputed path. Our backward smoother also evaluates the transition density
to sample that path. For Dhaka, a single Euler step is driven by one
Brownian increment and has no density with respect to Lebesgue measure
on the six-dimensional state space. Composing 20 Euler steps gives a
monthly transition that we can
simulate, but whose density we cannot evaluate directly after integrating
out the intermediate states. IF2 and IFAD use this simulator without
evaluating its transition density; IFAD additionally differentiates
through the simulation. They retain the Euler discretization error but
do not require a separate approximation to the monthly transition density.

We approximate the transition over each month by a Gaussian distribution.
The local strong-order-1.5 approximation has a covariance matrix of rank
at most two, so it does not have a density in the six-dimensional state
space. We instead calculate the mean and covariance over a full month by
propagating the second-order mean and covariance through 20 time steps.
We linearize the dynamics at each step and integrate out the intermediate
states. The resulting monthly covariance matrix can have full rank,
although it remains poorly conditioned. To stabilize the calculation,
we add $10^{-12}$ times its largest diagonal entry to each diagonal entry.
This gives a monthly transition density that differs from the Euler
approximation used to evaluate the final parameter estimates.

The rank deficiency also affects parameter updates on an augmented path.
At a fixed interior state, the range of the unregularized local covariance is
\[
 \operatorname{span}\left\{
 (-1,1,0,0,0,0)^{\mathsf T},\;
 (0,-\gamma-m,\gamma,0,0,m)^{\mathsf T}
 \right\}.
\]
This follows by writing the diffusion vector as $g=q(-1,1,0,0,0,0)^{\mathsf T}$:
its diffusion derivatives are parallel to $g$, while the drift derivative
$(Db)g$ supplies the second direction. The plane depends on $m/\gamma$.
Changing this ratio changes the support of the local Gaussian transition,
so augmenting the states at every internal step does not provide an
ordinary full-state density for parameter optimization. Changes in the
transition mean can also change its affine support. An augmentation in
terms of driving noise would require a different parameter-update scheme.
Our implementation adds the covariance floor only after composing the
monthly transition, not at each internal step.

There is a separate difficulty with finely augmented paths even when the
transition densities are nonsingular. As the time step decreases, transitions
become concentrated and observations at intermediate steps are uninformative,
which can make particle resampling and backward sampling ineffective
\citep{karppinen24}. This concerns mixing of the path sampler and does not
by itself establish mutual singularity of path laws at different parameter
values. Our smoother samples monthly endpoints, so it does not assess
this fine-grid behavior. We restrict the comparison to this implementable
adaptation of DS19; constructing and validating a different augmented-path
method is beyond the present comparison.

We use each month's observation to guide the particle filter toward states
that are consistent with the observed death count. For each particle, we
calculate the measurement variance using the predicted monthly death count
and hold this variance fixed when conditioning on the observation.
This gives a Gaussian distribution from which we sample the next state.
We weight each sample by the original measurement density multiplied by
the ratio of the transition density to the proposal density.

At each optimization step, we run the particle filter through the data,
then sample a trajectory of monthly states backward through the stored
particles. We differentiate the complete-data log-likelihood of this
trajectory and average the resulting scores using stochastic approximation.
For the 23-parameter Dhaka model, these searches use averaged-score ascent.
This differs from averaging and maximizing the complete-data objective in
SAEM. Section~\ref{sec:ds19-update-equations} gives both updates and describes
the separate numerical-SAEM comparison. We clip the scores and use
backtracking, with periodic checks of the log-likelihood under the monthly
Gaussian transition approximation. The 100- and 1,000-particle fits use
100 particles for these checks; the separately tuned 5,000-particle
experiment uses 5,000.
We also checked the particle filter on the harmonic oscillator example of
\citet{ditlevsen19}, for which an analytic Gaussian filter is available.
With 100 particles and 1,000 observations, the root mean squared difference
between the particle and analytic filtered means was 0.00523.

\paragraph{CTDD21.}
The method of \citet{corenflos21} uses entropy-regularized optimal transport
to make particle resampling differentiable. We implemented it using the
same Euler simulator as in the main text, with 20 steps per month.
The transport calculation uses all six state coordinates, scaled as in
the published implementation. We solve the transport problem using
Sinkhorn iterations, gradually decreasing the regularization parameter
to 0.25 with an annealing scale of 0.75. We use a convergence tolerance
of $10^{-3}$ and a maximum of 100 Sinkhorn iterations. To calculate
derivatives, we hold the converged potentials fixed and differentiate
through one further fixed-point update. As in the published implementation,
we clip each component of the derivative propagated through the transport
step to $[-1,1]$. We resample when the effective sample size falls below
half the number of particles.

We maximize the resulting log-likelihood estimate using Adam, with new
random numbers for the particle filter at each iteration and a gradient
norm limited to 100. If a search becomes invalid or repeatedly deteriorates,
we return to its best valid parameter estimate, reset the Adam moments,
and halve the learning rate. We allow at most six such restarts.
Entropic resampling changes the particle filter's likelihood approximation,
so the objective used for optimization can differ from the ordinary
particle filter estimate used to evaluate the results.

\subsection{Experimental settings}

For each method, we performed 100 global searches from starting parameter
vectors drawn uniformly from the same wide bounding box as in the main
text. We tested 100 and 1,000 particles, using learning rates of 0.02 for
CTDD21 and 0.10 for DS19. We allowed each search 800 seconds and
selected parameter estimates at approximately 700 seconds. For CTDD21,
we selected the saved estimate with the highest fitting objective before
700 seconds. If none was valid, we used another valid saved estimate or
the initial parameter vector. For DS19, we selected the first saved
estimate at or after 700 seconds, or the last estimate if the search ended
earlier. Thus a DS19 search could exceed the selection time by one
iteration.

To assess the benefit of an IF2 warm start, we also initialized both
methods at the 100 IF2 estimates used to initialize IFAD-0.97 in the main
text's comparable computational effort experiment. We used these estimates
directly, including those outside the initial bounding box. The IF2 stage
used 175 iterations and 5,000 particles, taking 92.160 seconds under the
timing procedure described in the main text. We then allowed each method
400 iterations or another 850.657 seconds, giving a total time limit of
942.817 seconds. The learning rates were 0.0002 for CTDD21 and 0.0005
for DS19, at both 100 and 1,000 particles. We selected the last valid
CTDD21 estimate before the time limit and the first DS19 estimate
at or after it, or the last estimate if the search ended earlier.

We kept the other optimization settings fixed when increasing the number
of particles from 100 to 1,000. The 1,000-particle experiments ran
sequentially on one RTX 3090 GPU, as did the 100-particle experiments
initialized by IF2. The 100-particle DS19 global searches ran four at
a time on that GPU. Consequently, both the particle count and GPU
scheduling affect the number of iterations completed within the time limit.
We also ran DS19 with 5,000 particles from the global starting points.
For that experiment, we separately tuned the learning rate to 0.20 and
began decreasing the stochastic approximation step size earlier.

We evaluated all selected parameter vectors independently using the Euler
approximation with 20 steps per month, including estimates from searches
that ended early. Each evaluation used 36 particle filter replicates with
5,000 particles. We averaged the likelihood estimates before taking the
logarithm. For the optimization progress plots, we also evaluated each
saved iterate using one replicate with 5,000 particles. These plots show
the iterates visited during optimization, which can differ from the
estimates selected for the final comparison. As in the main text, we
excluded the additional evaluations from the reported computation times.

\subsection{Results}

Our findings are summarized in Tables~\ref{tab:dhaka-competitors}
and~\ref{tab:dhaka-competitors-paired} and
Figures~\ref{fig:dhaka-competitors-final}--\ref{fig:dhaka-competitors-parameters}.
The figures show CTDD21 with 100 particles and DS19 with 1,000,
using the same particle count for each method with and without an IF2
warm start. IFAD-0.97 uses 5,000 particles. The tables include all particle
counts tested.

Both methods attained lower median log-likelihoods than IFAD-0.97 in the
global searches. At the particle counts shown in the figures, the medians
were $-4554.67$ for CTDD21 and $-3998.93$ for DS19, compared with
$-3744.40$ for IFAD-0.97. Increasing the number of particles did not
consistently improve performance within the allotted time. For CTDD21,
increasing from 100 to 1,000 particles reduced the median number of updates
from 255 to 28, and the median log-likelihood fell to $-6312.89$.
For DS19, the median improved from $-4100.34$ with 100 particles to
$-3998.93$ with 1,000 particles, while the median number of updates rose
from 55 to 147. This comparison also includes the change in GPU scheduling
described above. With 5,000 particles and separately tuned settings,
DS19 attained a median of $-4100.40$ after a median of 47 updates.

\begin{table}[htbp!]
\centering
\small
\input{imgs/competitors/results_table.tex}

warm start. Median and maximum use independent particle filter evaluations
with 20 Euler steps per month. Updates gives the median number of

Starting from IF2 estimates substantially improved the final log-likelihoods
of both methods, but neither consistently improved on the IF2 estimates
themselves. With 100 particles, CTDD21 improved on 45 of the 100 starting
estimates and DS19 improved on 18. The median changes were $-0.10$
and $-1.78$ log-likelihood units, respectively. With 1,000 particles, these
counts were 48 and 24, and the median changes were $-0.03$ and $-1.15$.
CTDD21 completed a median of 213 updates with 100 particles and 20 with
1,000 particles; DS19 completed 252 and 171, respectively.
IFAD-0.97 continued from the same IF2 estimates to a median log-likelihood
of $-3744.40$, compared with $-3766.64$ at the end of IF2.

For both methods and both particle counts, the median change in the fitting
objective was positive even though the median change in the independently
evaluated log-likelihood was negative (Table~\ref{tab:dhaka-competitors-paired}).
The two evaluations use different likelihood approximations: DS19
fits the monthly Gaussian transition model, while CTDD21 uses entropic
resampling. The fitting objectives are also estimated using a single
particle filter, and the updates depend on clipping and the numerical
safeguards described above. These experiments do not determine how much
each of these factors contributes to the disagreement.

\subsection{Reproducibility}

Source code and algorithmic settings are available at
\href{https://github.com/pypomp/dmop}{https://github.com/pypomp/dmop}, in the
\texttt{ditlevsen/} and \texttt{corenflos/} directories.
The file \path{imgs/competitors/README.md} gives instructions for reproducing
the tables and figures, identifies the numerical results used, and links
to plots showing the full range of log-likelihoods. The repository includes
the tables and figures needed to compile the supplement. Instructions for
retrieving archived parameter estimates and evaluations at individual
iterations are in \path{artifacts/README.md}.

\clearpage
\section{Comparisons Across Models}
\label{sec:cross-model-benchmarks}

We compared IFAD, IF2, DS19, and CTDD21 on the harmonic oscillator of
\citet{ditlevsen19}, the two-dimensional linear Gaussian model of
\citet{corenflos21}, and the Pypomp library implementation of a stochastic
volatility model at \texttt{pypomp.models.spx}, subsequently called
\texttt{spx}, in addition to the Daphnia and Dhaka models.
For each example, we used the same starting parameter vectors for all
methods and evaluated the resulting estimates under the same model.
The two Gaussian examples have analytic likelihoods, allowing us to
assess maximization error without Monte~Carlo evaluation error.

Table~\ref{tab:ds19-models} summarizes the DS19 implementation for each
panel. All five implementations evaluate process transition densities.
For the linear Gaussian model and \texttt{spx}, these
densities are available directly. For the oscillator, all methods fit
the same order-1.5 Gaussian approximation, whose likelihood we evaluate
by Kalman filtering. Thus these three comparisons use the same transition
model for fitting and evaluation.

For Daphnia and Dhaka, we also use moments from the order-1.5 expansion,
but compose them over smaller time steps and approximate the transition
between observations by a Gaussian distribution. This composition adds
a linearization of the nonlinear dynamics. It does not imply that the
resulting Gaussian approximation has strong order 1.5 over the full
observation interval. For Dhaka, a single local Gaussian approximation
has covariance rank at most two in six dimensions, as explained in
Section~\ref{sec:dhaka-competitors}. Composing the local covariances allows
noise to propagate into additional state directions. For Daphnia, we
compose moments at the 0.25-day simulation step rather than apply a
single Taylor expansion across the five-day observation interval.
We have not tested the accuracy of such a single coarse step.
Both models are evaluated using their original Euler simulators, so the
Gaussian approximation introduces a difference between the fitting and
evaluation likelihoods.
