\subsection{SAEM and averaged-score updates}
\label{sec:ds19-update-equations}

Stochastic approximation expectation maximization (SAEM) alternates
imputation of the latent states with maximization of an averaged
complete-data log-likelihood.
Let $x$ denote a latent trajectory and write
$C(\vartheta;x)=\log\widetilde p_\vartheta(x,y)$ for the complete-data
objective of the fitted model. The tilde allows for a transition-density
approximation. Deterministic initial states are functions of the candidate
parameter; only random states are imputed. At iteration $m$, a particle
filter followed by backward
simulation draws one trajectory $X^{(m)}$ from an approximation to the
smoothing distribution at $\vartheta_{m-1}$.
SAEM updates a function of the candidate parameter $\vartheta$:
\begin{align}
 \overline Q_m(\vartheta)
 &= (1-\rho_m)\overline Q_{m-1}(\vartheta)
       +\rho_m C(\vartheta;X^{(m)}),
 \label{eq:benchmark-saem-q}\\
 \vartheta_m &\in
       \mathop{\mathrm{argmax}}_{\vartheta}\overline Q_m(\vartheta).
 \label{eq:benchmark-saem-m}
\end{align}
In the maximization step, we hold the imputed paths and their averaging
weights fixed. This maximization can be carried out numerically; a step
that increases $\overline Q_m$ without maximizing it gives a generalized
EM update.

The averaged-score implementation instead calculates
\begin{align}
 g_m &= \left.\nabla_\vartheta C(\vartheta;X^{(m)})
                         \right|_{\vartheta=\vartheta_{m-1}},
 \label{eq:benchmark-path-score}\\
 \overline g_m &=(1-\rho_m)\overline g_{m-1}+\rho_m g_m,
 \label{eq:benchmark-score-average}
\end{align}
then uses the clipped $\overline g_m$ in an Adam step.
Derivatives hold the imputed path fixed; they do not pass through the
particle filter or backward sampler.
With exact smoothing, the conditional expectation of $g_m$ is the score
of the fitted model's observed-data likelihood.
The particle smoother approximates this expectation.
Before clipping or safeguard resets, the distinction between the two
averages can be written as
\begin{align}
 \nabla\overline Q_m(\vartheta)
   &=\sum_{r=1}^m w_{mr}\nabla_\vartheta C(\vartheta;X^{(r)}),
 &\overline g_m
   &=\sum_{r=1}^m w_{mr}
       \nabla_\vartheta C(\vartheta_{r-1};X^{(r)}),
 \label{eq:benchmark-q-versus-score}\\
 w_{mr}&=\rho_r\prod_{k=r+1}^m(1-\rho_k).\nonumber
\end{align}
The SAEM gradient evaluates all path contributions at the same candidate
parameter, whereas $\overline g_m$ averages gradients evaluated at the
parameter values used in earlier iterations. Consequently,
$\overline g_m$ generally differs from the gradient of $\overline Q_m$
at the current parameter.
Our gain schedule is $\rho_m=1$ for $m\leq b$ and
$\rho_m=(m-b)^{-0.9}$ thereafter.
We use $b=30$ for the Gaussian SAEM and averaged-score runs, and $b=3$
for numerical SAEM on Dhaka.
Clipping and Adam act on the existing estimation scales.
In the Dhaka score implementation, the clipped average is also carried
into the next iteration, and failed safeguard checks can reset it.

\paragraph{Linear Gaussian model: explicit SAEM.}
The latent states satisfy
$X_t=\operatorname{diag}(a_1,a_2)X_{t-1}+\varepsilon_t$,
with $\varepsilon_t\sim\mathcal N(0,0.5I)$,
$Y_t\mid X_t\sim\mathcal N(X_t,0.1I)$, and
$X_0\sim\mathcal N(0,I)$.
For a fixed path, the terms depending on $a=(a_1,a_2)$ are
\[
 C(a;x)=-\frac{1}{2(0.5)}
     \sum_{t=1}^T\sum_{d=1}^2(x_{t,d}-a_dx_{t-1,d})^2
     +\text{constant}.
\]
For each coordinate, the sufficient statistics are
\[
 S_{00,d}(x)=\sum_t x_{t-1,d}^2,
 \qquad S_{10,d}(x)=\sum_t x_{t,d}x_{t-1,d}.
\]
We update their averages by
$\overline S_m=(1-\rho_m)\overline S_{m-1}+\rho_m S(X^{(m)})$.
The M-step is
\begin{equation}
 a_{d,m}=\operatorname{clip}_{[-0.98,0.98]}
       \left(\frac{\overline S_{10,d,m}}{\overline S_{00,d,m}}\right).
 \label{eq:benchmark-linear-mstep}
\end{equation}
Each iteration therefore requires a particle filter and backward
simulation of one path, after which the maximization reduces to two
scalar ratios.

\paragraph{Harmonic oscillator: SAEM with a numerical M-step.}
Write $x_t=(v_t,u_t)^\mathsf T$, where the positions $v_t$ are observed
without measurement error and the velocities $u_t$ are imputed.
For $\theta=(D,\gamma,\sigma)$ and observation interval $\Delta=0.02$,
the fitted Gaussian transition has
\begin{align*}
 A_\theta&=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\-D&-\gamma\end{pmatrix},
 &F_\theta&=I+\Delta A_\theta+\tfrac12\Delta^2 A_\theta^2,\\
 Q_\theta&=\sigma^2
 \begin{pmatrix}
 \Delta^3/3&\Delta^2/2-\gamma\Delta^3/3\\
 \Delta^2/2-\gamma\Delta^3/3&
       \Delta-\gamma\Delta^2+\gamma^2\Delta^3/3
 \end{pmatrix}.
\end{align*}
Conditional on the first position, $U_0\sim\mathcal N(0,v_\theta)$,
where $v_\theta=\sigma^2/(2\gamma)$.
Let $S_{ij}=\sum_{t=1}^T x_{t-1+i}x_{t-1+j}^\mathsf T$ for
$(i,j)=(0,0),(1,0),(1,1)$, and let $h=u_0^2$.
We average these quantities using the recursion above, giving the
following objective up to constants independent of $\theta$:
\begin{equation}
 \overline Q_m(\theta)=-\tfrac12\left\{
  \log v_\theta+\frac{\overline h_m}{v_\theta}
  +T\log\det Q_\theta
  +\operatorname{tr}\!\left(Q_\theta^{-1}\overline R_m(\theta)\right)
 \right\},
 \label{eq:benchmark-oscillator-mstep}
\end{equation}
where
\[
 \overline R_m(\theta)=\overline S_{11,m}
     -F_\theta\overline S_{10,m}^{\mathsf T}
     -\overline S_{10,m}F_\theta^{\mathsf T}
     +F_\theta\overline S_{00,m}F_\theta^{\mathsf T}.
\]
We use L-BFGS-B on $(\log D,\log\gamma,\log\sigma)$, retaining a finite
step only if this objective does not decrease, up to numerical tolerance.
To reduce numerical cancellation, we evaluate this quadratic form using
accumulated state increments. The M-step thus uses averaged sufficient
statistics of the imputed paths.

\paragraph{SPX: averaged complete-data scores.}
Let $r_t$ be the observed return and $v_t$ the latent variance.
The \texttt{spx} conditional variance transition has Gaussian mean
and standard deviation
\[
 m_t(v)=v+\kappa(\bar v-v)+\xi\rho(r_{t-1}-\mu+v/2),
 \qquad s(v)=\xi\sqrt{v(1-\rho^2)},
\]
followed by a floor $\epsilon=10^{-32}$.
Writing $\phi(z;m,s^2)$ for a Gaussian density and $\Phi$ for the
standard normal distribution function, its transition is
\[
 f_{\theta,t}(v'\mid v)=
 \begin{cases}
  \phi(v';m_t(v),s(v)^2),&v'>\epsilon,\\
  \Phi((\epsilon-m_t(v))/s(v)),&v'=\epsilon.
 \end{cases}
\]
The second case is a probability mass; the reference measure is Lebesgue
measure plus an atom at $\epsilon$.
The complete-path objective is
\begin{equation}
 C(\theta;v)=\sum_{t=1}^T\left\{
    \log f_{\theta,t}(v_t\mid v_{t-1})
    +\log\phi(r_t;\mu-v_t/2,v_t)\right\}.
 \label{eq:benchmark-spx-score-objective}
\end{equation}
The initial variance $v_0=V_0$ is a parameter, so its current candidate
value enters the first transition when differentiating.
For $v>0$, $\xi>0$, and $|\rho|<1$, the conditional transition variance
$\xi^2v(1-\rho^2)$ is positive. This scalar latent process therefore has
no missing covariance direction in the interior; its boundary atom is
included explicitly in the density above.
For this example, we update the parameters with Adam using the averaged
scores in Equations~(\ref{eq:benchmark-path-score})--(\ref{eq:benchmark-score-average}),
without a SAEM maximization step.
We obtain these complete-data derivatives by AD while holding the imputed
path fixed. This avoids differentiation through the filtering and resampling
calculations, but does not avoid AD itself. The runtime difference from
IFAD also reflects the particle counts and optimization settings below.

\paragraph{Daphnia: averaged scores for the Gaussian block approximation.}
For mesocosm $u$, let $m_{\theta,u,t}$ and $\Sigma_{\theta,u,t}$ be the
mean and covariance obtained by composing the local Gaussian transitions
over an observation interval, including the inoculation in the first interval.
Let $g_{\theta,u}$ be the product of the four negative-binomial measurement
probabilities in Section~S10.
For the eight imputed mesocosm paths, the complete-path objective is
\begin{equation}
 C(\theta;x)=\sum_{u=1}^{8}\sum_{t=1}^{10}
 \left\{\log\phi_8\!\left(x_{u,t};
 m_{\theta,u,t}(x_{u,t-1}),\Sigma_{\theta,u,t}(x_{u,t-1})\right)
       +\log g_{\theta,u}(y_{u,t}\mid x_{u,t})\right\}.
 \label{eq:benchmark-daphnia-score-objective}
\end{equation}
Initial states are fixed. The covariance uses a relative diagonal floor
of $10^{-8}$ on scaled states. The particle filter uses the observations
to guide proposals, with the original negative-binomial measurement
probabilities in the importance weights.
We approximate each observation interval by one eight-dimensional
Gaussian transition, composing local moments over 24 steps for the first
interval and 20 thereafter. The step size is 0.25 days, so these
transitions cover six days initially and five days between observations.
This approximates the distribution produced by the nonlinear dynamics
over the whole interval. Boundary handling also differs: we assign zero
weight to Gaussian proposals outside the allowed state range, whereas
the Euler simulator resets an offending state to zero and assigns a
log weight of $-150$ to that particle at the next observation.
Both differences can change the likelihood being maximized.
The local covariance need not be rank deficient in this model.
At a strictly positive state, the six independent noises span the six
directly forced coordinates. The integrated juvenile noises contribute
two further directions through maturation into susceptible adults, with
nonzero coefficients $\lambda_J\sigma_J^{\mathrm n}J^{\mathrm n}$ and
$\lambda_J\sigma_J^{\mathrm i}J^{\mathrm i}$.
The implemented order-1.5 covariance is therefore full rank in this
interior when the six fitted noise coefficients are positive, and its
range is independent of the parameter values there. At the specified
initial state several populations are zero, and the local covariance has
rank three. Daphnia thus differs from Dhaka's rank-two interior covariance.
The algebra and numerical checks, including parameter perturbations at
fixed states, are provided in \path{code/benchmarks/covariance_support.py}
and its accompanying note.
After backward simulation of one path per mesocosm, we differentiate this
sum with respect to the 38 estimated parameters and update them with
Adam using the averaged scores. As for SPX, we do not carry out a SAEM
maximization step. We evaluate the resulting parameter estimates using
the original Euler model from Section~S10.

\paragraph{Dhaka: score updates and numerical generalized SAEM.}
We compare score updates with numerical SAEM using the monthly Gaussian transition described in
Section~\ref{sec:dhaka-competitors}, with 20 internal time steps and
a relative covariance floor of $10^{-12}$.
Let $\mathcal R x$ reset the accumulated monthly deaths to zero, and
write $d_t=P_tM_t$ for the predicted deaths at a sampled endpoint.
Here $P_t$ is the population and $M_t$ is the accumulated death count
divided by population. Both methods use the complete-data objective
\begin{align}
 C(\theta;x)=\sum_{t=1}^T\big\{&
   \log\phi_6\!\left(x_t;m_{\theta,t}(\mathcal R x_{t-1}),
                     \Sigma_{\theta,t}(\mathcal R x_{t-1})\right)
   \nonumber\\[-2pt]
   &+\log[\phi(y_t;d_t,(\tau d_t+\delta)^2)+\delta]\big\},
 \qquad \delta=10^{-18}.
 \label{eq:benchmark-dhaka-objective}
\end{align}
The initial state $x_0=x_0(\theta)$ is recalculated at each candidate
parameter, while the sampled monthly endpoints are held fixed.
For the score updates, we use the gradient average in
Equation~(\ref{eq:benchmark-score-average}), with Adam and the safeguards
described above.
For numerical SAEM, we retain the paths and weights to calculate
$\overline Q_m(\theta)=\sum_{r=1}^m w_{mr}C(\theta;X^{(r)})$.
Each L-BFGS-B evaluation recalculates every retained path's objective
and gradient at the same candidate $\theta$.
Within each M-step, we divide the objective and its gradient by the fixed
scale $\max\{1,\|\nabla\overline Q_m(\theta_{m-1})\|_\infty\}$.
This reduces numerical line-search failures without changing the maximizer.
We allow at most 25 L-BFGS-B iterations per M-step and retain a candidate
only if it is finite and increases or leaves unchanged this fixed
$\overline Q_m$. An M-step interrupted by the time limit is discarded.
This gives a generalized-SAEM update when an explicit maximizer in terms
of sufficient statistics is unavailable.
We evaluate both methods with the Euler likelihood, so the evaluation
model differs from the monthly Gaussian approximation used for fitting.

\paragraph{Computation times for the Gaussian examples.}
We used 500 particles for DS19, IFAD, and IF2 in both Gaussian examples.
DS19 ran for 80 SAEM iterations, IFAD for 100 IF2 iterations followed by
300 gradient iterations, and IF2 for 600 iterations.
CTDD21 used 25 particles and 300 gradient iterations.
The common particle count for DS19, IFAD, and IF2 therefore does not
imply equal computational effort.

DS19 runs a particle filter and samples one trajectory by backward
simulation at every iteration, and both are included in its computation
time. Its maximization step is inexpensive for these models: the linear
Gaussian model has an explicit update, while the oscillator requires
optimization over three parameters using averaged sufficient statistics.
Neither maximization requires differentiation through the particle filter.
The analytic likelihoods are used only to evaluate the fitted parameters.
For all methods, we wait for the numerical calculations to finish before
recording the time, excluding compilation and likelihood evaluation.
IFAD, IF2, and DS19 used four efficiency cores of an Intel Core i9-13900K;
CTDD21 used two performance cores with four hardware threads.
Concurrent batches used separate core groups. These hardware differences
also affect the comparison of computation times.

\subsection{Search and evaluation settings}
\label{sec:benchmark-settings}

We ran 20 searches per method for the oscillator, linear Gaussian, SPX,
and Daphnia models, using the same starting parameter vectors for each
method. For Dhaka, we used the 100 searches from
Section~\ref{sec:dhaka-competitors}.

For SPX, we used 500 particles for IFAD and IF2, 100 for DS19, and 25
for CTDD21. IFAD used $\alpha=0.97$ and ran for 100 IF2 iterations followed by 300 gradient
iterations, IF2 for 600 iterations, and DS19 and CTDD21 for 300 updates
each. The Adam learning rates started at $0.001$ for IFAD and $0.01$
for DS19 and CTDD21, decreasing to one tenth of these values by cosine decay.

For Daphnia, we used 1,000 particles per mesocosm for MPIF and IFAD,
and 100 for DS19 and CTDD21. We ran MPIF for 680 iterations.
IFAD used a separate 200-iteration MPIF warm start followed by 200
gradient steps. To compare refinement of the same starting estimate,
we initialized DS19 and CTDD21 at this MPIF warm start and allowed each
the time taken by the IFAD gradient stage, with a maximum of 2,000 updates.
Their learning rates decreased from $0.001$ to $0.0001$ by cosine decay
over the allotted time. We discarded updates completed after the time
limit and included the common MPIF warm start in the computation times
for all three methods.

We evaluated the final SPX estimates using 24 independent particle-filter
replicates with 5,000 particles. For Daphnia, we used 10 replicates with
2,000 particles per mesocosm under the original Euler model.
We averaged the likelihood estimates for each mesocosm before taking
logarithms, then summed the log-likelihoods over mesocosms, combining
their independent Monte~Carlo errors.
For the optimization trajectories, we used four replicates with 1,000
particles for SPX and two replicates with 500 particles per mesocosm
for Daphnia. All these evaluations were independent of fitting;
we did not use them to select the reported parameter estimates.

SPX fits used two performance cores with four hardware threads, except
for one recovered starting point for which all four methods used four
efficiency cores. Daphnia fits used four efficiency cores.
These runs used the same processor as the Gaussian comparisons.
The archived Dhaka comparison used GPU computations as described in
Section~\ref{sec:dhaka-competitors}.

\subsection{Repeated-search results}

IFAD and DS19 both approached the maximum likelihood for the two
Gaussian models (Table~\ref{tab:benchmark-results} and
Figure~\ref{fig:benchmark-final-likelihoods}). DS19 did so more quickly,
benefiting from the inexpensive maximization steps described above.
IF2 gave more variable results, while CTDD21 attained lower likelihoods,
particularly for the oscillator. DS19 also had the highest median
log-likelihood and shortest median computation time for SPX.
We therefore found no consistent improvement from IFAD on these examples.

For Daphnia, IFAD attained the highest maximum and median log-likelihoods.
The median was $-861.30$, compared with $-864.98$ for MPIF, $-867.02$
for DS19, and $-865.03$ for CTDD21.
Figure~\ref{fig:benchmark-optimization-detail} shows IFAD continuing to
improve after the common MPIF warm start, while DS19 and CTDD21 made
less progress with the same time allowance.
The full trajectories are shown in Figure~\ref{fig:benchmark-optimization-full};
the shaded regions in both figures cover the 10th through 100th
percentiles, as in Section~\ref{sec:dhaka-competitors}.
We retained the last finite estimate from one DS19 search that stopped
prematurely. For Dhaka, the differences were larger: IFAD attained a
median log-likelihood of $-3744.40$, compared with $-3755.41$ for IF2,
$-3769.04$ for DS19, and $-3765.91$ for CTDD21.

The Daphnia comparison does not determine how much of the DS19 shortfall
comes from the Gaussian transition approximation. Smoothing error and
optimization may also contribute. DS19 used 100 particles per mesocosm,
compared with 1,000 for IFAD, and estimated each gradient from one
backward-sampled path per mesocosm for the 38-parameter optimization.
Moreover, the Daphnia implementation uses averaged-score Adam updates,
whereas the Gaussian examples use SAEM maximization with sufficient
statistics. We have not compared these updates with numerical SAEM on
Daphnia. Evaluating both the Gaussian fitting objective and the Euler
likelihood along the same searches would help assess the effect of the
transition approximation: improvement in the former accompanied by
deterioration in the latter would indicate that improving the approximate
objective need not improve the likelihood of interest. We have not
carried out this diagnostic.

The Gaussian examples provide useful numerical checks because their
likelihoods are available analytically. Once methods repeatedly approach
these maxima, the examples offer limited evidence about further
improvements in likelihood maximization. The SPX comparison still shows
differences between methods and is not saturated in this sense.
Daphnia and Dhaka retain difficulties encountered in scientific
applications, including many estimated parameters, nonlinear dynamics,
partially observed states, and, for Daphnia, multiple experimental units.
Assessing methods for such applications requires benchmarks that retain
these difficulties alongside tractable examples used to verify implementations.

\subsection{Score updates and numerical SAEM on Dhaka}

To assess whether the score updates accounted for the poor performance
of DS19 on Dhaka, we repeated the comparison using numerical SAEM with
the same monthly Gaussian transition approximation and particle smoother.
We used 1,000 particles and allowed 850 seconds per search, starting
from 20 global parameter vectors and 20 IF2 estimates.
Numerical SAEM gave higher Euler log-likelihoods than the score updates
for only two of the 20 global starts
(Figure~\ref{fig:dhaka-saem-ab} and Table~\ref{tab:dhaka-saem-ab}).
The median paired difference was $-608.83$ log-likelihood units, taking
numerical SAEM minus score updates. The numerical M-steps were expensive:
SAEM completed a median of 15.5 outer iterations in the allotted time,
compared with 86 for the score updates.

Starting from the IF2 estimates reduced this difference substantially.
The median paired difference was $-0.62$ log-likelihood units, although
one SAEM search finished $39.59$ units below its score counterpart.
SAEM gave higher likelihoods in four of the 20 pairs.
Neither method improved on the IF2 estimates at the median, with changes
of $-0.27$ for score updates and $-1.37$ for numerical SAEM.
Thus numerical maximization of the SAEM objective did not improve the
Dhaka results.
Both methods retained the same monthly Gaussian transition and particle
smoother, so this comparison does not measure the effect of the transition
approximation. Increasing the number of particles can improve the
particle approximation to this Gaussian model, but does not remove its
difference from the Euler model. The results therefore leave open the
relative contributions of transition approximation, smoothing error,
numerical conditioning, and optimization cost to the Dhaka shortfall.
